Algorithms for hard-constraint point processes via discretization
Tobias Friedrich ∗, Andreas Göbel ∗, Maximilian Katzmann ∗, Martin S. Krejca †, Marcus Pappik ∗

Abstract
We study the algorithmic applications of a natural discretization for the hard-sphere model and the Widom–Rowlinson model in a region of d d -dimensional Euclidean space 𝕍 ⊂ ℝ d \mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d} . These continuous models are frequently used in statistical physics to describe mixtures of one or multiple particle types subjected to hard-core interactions. For each type, particles are distributed according to a Poisson point process with a type specific activity parameter, called fugacity . The Gibbs distribution over all possible system states is characterized by the mixture of these point processes conditioned that no two particles being closer than some type-dependent distance threshold. A key part in better understanding the Gibbs distribution is its normalizing constant, called partition function . We give sufficient conditions that the partition function of a discrete hard-core model on a geometric graph based on a point set X ⊂ 𝕍 X\subset\mathds{V} closely approximates those of such continuous models. Previously, such a result was only shown for the hard-sphere model on cubic regions 𝕍 = [ 0 , ℓ ) d \mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d} when the size of X X is exponential in the volume of the region OPEN ν ​ ( 𝕍 CLOSE ) \nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right) , limiting algorithmic applications. In the same setting, our refined analysis only requires a quadratic number of points, which we argue to be tight. We use our improved discretization results to approximate the partition functions of the hard-sphere model and the Widom-Rowlinson efficiently in OPEN ν ​ ( 𝕍 CLOSE ) \nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right) . For simplicity, we state our algorithmic results for cubic regions although most of our technical arguments extend to broader classes of regions. For the hard-sphere model, we obtain the first quasi-polynomial deterministic approximation algorithm for the entire fugacity regime for which, so far, only randomized approximations are known. Furthermore, we simplify a recently introduced fully polynomial randomized approximation algorithm. For high dimensions, the fugacity regime we achieve is consistent with the best known bound for uniqueness of the Gibbs measure. Similarly, we obtain the best known approximation bounds for the Widom–Rowlinson model, both randomized and deterministically. Moreover, we obtain efficient approximate samplers for the Gibbs distributions of the respective spin systems within the same fugacity regimes.

\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\bullet$}}}}

\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\bullet$}}}}

\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\bullet$}}}}

\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\bullet$}}}}

\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.6}{$\bullet$}}}}

1
Introduction

Statistical physics models complex systems of interacting particles as probability distributions.
The main goal is to explain the macroscopic properties of such a
spin system
when it is only described by the microscopic interactions among its particles.
Two classical models in this area are the hard-sphere model, central in the analysis of thermodynamics of liquids and liquid mixtures
[
HM13
,
BNH80
]
, and the Widom–Rowlinson model, which explains the evaporation of liquids
[
WR70
]
.
These models have in common, that each of their states consists of a finite set of points in Euclidean space, distributed according to a Poisson point process.
The points correspond to centers of spherical particles and are constrained by hard-core interactions, i.e., two particles are not allowed to occupy the same space.
The probability distribution characterizing the valid states of a model is called its
Gibbs distribution
.
A key part in better understanding this distribution is its normalizing factor: the
partition function
of the model.

Statistical physics studies spin systems in terms of their
phase transitions
, i.e., the change of the system’s macroscopic behavior as one of its parameters crosses a critical value.
Rigorous proofs of phase transitions for
continuous
spin systems are rare.
Concerning our previous examples, a phase transition has been proved to exist for the Widom–Rowlinson model
[
Rue71
]
, while it is still an open question for the hard-sphere model.
Phase transitions are more commonly shown for
discrete
spin systems, where, instead of the Euclidean space, particles are placed on the vertices of a graph and interact only with particles on adjacent vertices.

One of the simplest and most well-studied discrete spin systems is the
hard-core model
.
It is considered to be a universal model, as any discrete spin system can be mapped to a hard-core instance
[
Sim93
, Section 5.7]
.
For an undirected graph
G=(V,E)
and a function
\lambda\colon V\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
, the hard-core model is defined by the tuple
(G,\lambda)
.
Its set of valid states is the set of all independent sets
\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
of
G
.
Each independent set
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
is assigned the weight
\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
.
The partition function
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)
of the hard-core model is the sum of weights of all independent sets, and its Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)}
assigns each independent set a probability proportional to its weight, normalized over the partition function.
Formally,

Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\text{ and, for all $I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)$, we have }\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(I}}\right)=\frac{\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}{Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)}.

In the uniform case, where
\lambda
is constant, classical results in statistical physics establish the existence of a threshold for which the hard-core model undergoes a phase transition on various graph classes, such as two-dimensional lattices
[
LY52
]
and regular trees
[
Kel85
,
BW99
]
.
In a series of celebrated results, the tree threshold has been linked to a change in the computational behavior of the hard-core model.
That is, for values of
\lambda
where the Gibbs measure is unique on a
\Delta
-regular tree, there is a polynomial-time algorithm for approximating
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)
on general graphs of maximum degree
\Delta

[
Wei06
]
, while for the remaining values of
\lambda
, this is
NP
-hard
[
Sly10
,
GGS+14
]
.

As discrete models are better understood, the hard-core model on lattices is often used as a coarse-grained version of the continuous hard-sphere model.

[
FV17
]
write for this discretization:

“Although it might appear as a significant departure from reality, we will see that it leads to satisfactory results and allows a good qualitative understanding of the corresponding phenomena.”
[
FV17
, Chapter
4.1
]

In this article, we show that a natural discretization based on the hard-core model leads not only to a qualitative but also to an
algorithmic
understanding of the hard-sphere model and the Widom–Rowlinson model.
Our main contributions are:

•

We study a natural discretization of the continuous Widom–Rowlinson model and the hard-sphere model on a region
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
, resulting in an instance of the hard-core model
(G,\lambda)
.
More precisely, we determine conditions such that the hard-core partition function
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)
closely approximates the partition function of the continuous point process and the number of vertices of
G
is polynomial in the volume of
\mathds{V}
.

•

We obtain new algorithmic results for these continuous spin systems by combining our discretization result with known algorithms for the hard-core model.
We present sufficient conditions for randomized approximation algorithms of the partition function and sampling algorithms for the Gibbs distribution with running time polynomial in the volume of
\mathds{V}
.
For the Widom–Rowlinson model, this results in the best known parameter regime for approximation and sampling.
Under the same conditions, we show the existence of deterministic approximation algorithms for the partition function with running time quasi-polynomial in the volume of
\mathds{V}
.
As a result, we obtain the first efficient deterministic approximation algorithm for both models in the considered parameter regime.

•

We show that, under suitable conditions, the partition functions of the hard-sphere model and the Widom–Rowlinson model can be seen as concentration points of hard-core partition functions on certain families of geometric random graphs.
Further, we explore the asymptotic behavior of the concentration in terms of the number of vertices under different conditions on the region
\mathds{V}
.

1.1
Hard-constraint point processes

As discussed earlier, the hard-sphere model and the Widom–Rowlinson model have in common that they are characterized by a point process with hard-core interactions in a region
\mathds{V}
.
To remove redundancy, we define a slightly more general class of models that includes both of the above.
We refer to this class as
hard-constraint point processes
.
Once we establish our results for this class of models, the corresponding statements for the hard-sphere model and the Widom–Rowlinson model follow immediately.

Let
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
be bounded and measurable, and let
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
.
The model represents the distribution of particles of
q
types, labeled by elements in
[q]\coloneqq[1,q]\cap\mathds{N}
, on
\mathds{V}
.
Particles of the same types are assumed to be indistinguishable.
Let
\lambda\colon[q]\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
be a function that equips each particle type with a fugacity.
For each particle type
i\in[q]
, we assume that the positions of particles are distributed according to a (labeled) Poisson point process of intensity
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
on
\mathds{V}
.
To add the constraints to the model, let
R\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}^{q\times q}
be a symmetric
q\times q
matrix, called the
interaction matrix
.
We condition the mixture of point processes by rejecting all configurations that contain particles at positions
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathds{V}
with corresponding particle types
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in[q]
with distance
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},x_{2}}}\right)<R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}}\right)
.
That is, the entries of
R
determine the minimum distance that particles of the respective types can have.
Especially note that
R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}}\right)=0
means that the particle types
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in[q]
are not subjected to any pairwise constraints.
Thus, if all entries of
R
are set to
0
, we obtain a simple mixture of Poisson point processes.

For any instance
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
of a hard-constraint point process, the above characterizes a
Gibbs distribution
as follows.
For all
k\in\mathds{N}
and all type assignments
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
, let
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{R}^{d}}}\right)^{k}\to\{0,1\}
be the function that indicates for a tuple of particles positions
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in[k]}\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{R}^{d}}}\right)^{k}
whether it forms a valid configuration, assuming that for each
i\in[k]
, the particle at
x_{i}
is of type
\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
.
Formally,

D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]\colon\\
i<j\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i},x_{j}}}\right)\geq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}}\right)}}\right\}},

noting that
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=1
for the case that
k=0
.
For each tuple
(\bm{x},\tau)
as above, the Gibbs distribution of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
is defined via the probability density

\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)=\frac{\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}{Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)},

where the normalizing constant
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
is called the
(grand canonical) partition function:

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)=1+\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k},

with
\nu^{d\times k}
denoting the product of
k
Lebesgue measures, each on
\mathds{R}^{d}
.
We proceed by showing how the hard-sphere model and the Widom–Rowlinson model are recovered from this framework.

Hard-sphere model:

The hard-sphere model describes the distribution of particles of a single type subjected to hard-core interactions.
It is parameterized by a radius
r\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
and a single fugacity
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Informally speaking, it is a simple Poisson point process of intensity
\lambda
, conditioned on no two points being closer than
2r
(i.e., when placing balls of radius
r
at each of the points, they must be non-overlapping).
Using our framework, this is equivalent to a hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R_{\text{HS}},\lambda)
with
q=1
particle types, where we slightly abuse notation and treat
\lambda
as a constant function, and set
R_{\text{HS}}
to be a
1\times 1
matrix containing only the entry
2r
.

Widom–Rowlinson model:

We consider the most general version of the Widom–Rowlinson model, although we might impose certain restrictions for some of our algorithmic results.
The Widom–Rowlinson model describes the interaction of particles of
q\geq 1
types, each equipped with a radius
r_{i}\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
and a fugacity
\lambda_{i}\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
for
i\in[q]
.
Informally speaking, the resulting distribution is a mixture of
q
Poisson point processes, each with its own intensity
\lambda_{i}
, with the condition that particles of the same type can be arbitrarily close to each other, but particles of different types
i,j\in[q]
need to have a distance of at least
r_{i}+r_{j}
(i.e., when placing a ball of radius
r_{i}
at each point of type
i\in[q]
, balls of different types must be non-overlapping).
This is equivalent to a hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R_{\text{WR}},\lambda)
with
q
particle types, where we set
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)=\lambda_{i}
for all
i\in[q]
, and, for all
i,j\in[q]
,

R_{\text{WR}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $i=j$,}\\
r_{i}+r_{j}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

1.2
Reduction to a discrete hard-core model

We investigate the following natural discretization to turn a (continuous) hard-constraint point process into a (discrete) hard-core model.
Given an instance of a hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types and a finite non-empty set of points
X\subset\mathds{V}
, we construct a simple undirected graph
G_{X}=(V_{X},E_{X})
as follows.
For each point in
X
and each type, we have a vertex in
V_{X}
.
Two distinct vertices are connected by an edge if no two particles of the corresponding types are allowed to occupy the respective positions in a valid configuration.
Consequently, independent sets of the graph correspond to valid particle configurations.
We aim to ‘simulate’ the original continuous model by a hard-core model on that graph.

Formally, we get the following construction:

•

For each point
x\in X
and each type
i\in[q]
, we construct a vertex
v_{x}^{(i)}
.
Furthermore, for each
i\in[q]
, we set
V_{X}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}=\big\{v_{x}^{(i)}\ \big|\ x\in X\big\}
, and we define
V_{X}=\bigcup_{i\in[q]}V_{X}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}
.

•

For each
i,j\in[q]
and
x,y\in X
, we connect
v_{x}^{(i)},v_{y}^{(j)}\in V_{X}
with an edge in
E_{X}
if

\neg(x=y\text{ and }i=j)\text{ and }d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)<R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right),

where the condition
\neg(x=y\text{ and }i=j)
prevents self-loops.

Additionally, we define a function
\lambda_{X}\colon V_{X}\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
such that, for all
i\in[q]
and all
x\in X
,
\lambda_{X}\big(v_{x}^{(i)}\big)=\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
.
Note that for all
i\in[q]
and all
x,y\in X
, this means that
\lambda_{X}\big(v_{x}^{(i)}\big)=\lambda_{X}\big(v_{y}^{(i)}\big)
, and we sometimes abuse notation and write
\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
instead.
We call
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
the
hard-core representation
of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
based on
X
.
Our goal is to find conditions, such that the hard-core partition function
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
closely approximates the partition function of original point process
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
.

To this end, we focus on sets
X
that are the result of a function
\Phi\colon\mathds{V}\to X
, which we call an
allocation.

More precisely, we study allocations
\Phi\colon\mathds{V}\rightarrow X
such that for each
x\in X
, the set
\Phi^{-1}(x)
has roughly the same volume and a bounded diameter.
This is expressed by the following definition.

Definition 1.1
(
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation).

Let
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
be bounded and measurable, and assume
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)>0
.
For finite
X\subset\mathds{V}
,
\delta\in[0,1)
, and
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, we call a function
\Phi\colon\mathds{V}\to X
a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation
for
X
if, for all
x\in X
,

1.

\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
is measurable and

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\leq\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\textrm{, and,}

2.

for all
y\in\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
, it holds that
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)\leq\varepsilon
.

Our main technical result is that, if the considered region
\mathds{V}
is star-convex,
-0.1
-0.1

-0.1

A region
\mathds{V}
is star convex if there is a center
x\in\mathds{V}
such that for every
y\in\mathds{V}
the straight line from
x
to
y
is contained in
\mathds{V}
.
the partition function of the hard-core representation based on a point set
X\subset\mathds{V}
closely approximates the partition function of the respective hard-constraint point process, whenever there is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
with sufficiently small
\delta
and
\varepsilon
.

(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types, and assume
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
is star-convex.
Furthermore, set
R_{\min}=\inf_{i,j\in[q]}\{R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\mid R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0\}
,

\inf\emptyset=\infty
, which in this setting means that
R_{\min}
can be chosen arbitrarily large.
and set

.
Let

with

.

Since
Footnote

-0.1
is at the core of our algorithmic results, we briefly overview its proof.
Given
X\subset\mathds{V}
and a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation
\Phi
, for each
x\in X
, the total weight that
\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
contributes to
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
is assigned to the total weight that the vertex of
G_{X}
that corresponds to
x
contributes to
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
.
This weight allocation introduces three types of errors between the two partition functions, which we all bound.
The first one is due to the Poisson point process of the continuous model potentially generating more than one point in
\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
, for some
x\in X
, which cannot be represented as part of the hard-core partition function.
To bound this error, we reduce the continuous model to an intermediate multiset version of the hard-core model, where valid configurations are allowed to contain multiple copies of the same vertex.
Then, we bound the difference between the partition function of the multiset hard-core model and
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
.
The second error type is due to the regions
\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
not necessarily having the same volume.
However, this error is small, due to
\delta
.
The third error type is due to configurations that are valid for the hard-constraint point process potentially being allocated to configurations that are invalid for the hard-core model and vice versa.
This happens because, e.g., two points
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathds{V}
of type
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
, respectively, with
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},x_{2}}}\right)\geq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}}\right)
get allocated to
\Phi(x_{1}),\Phi(x_{2})\in X
with
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi(x_{1}),\Phi(x_{2})}}\right)<R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}}\right)
.
The parameter
\varepsilon
ensures that each point
x\in\mathds{V}
is allocated to a close by point
\Phi(x)\in X
.
This allows us to bound this error by the difference of two partition functions with slightly different values in their exclusion matrices
R
.
In Euclidean space, we can express this as a difference between partition functions with the original exclusion matrix
R
but on differently scaled versions of the regions
(1\pm\alpha)\mathds{V}
for some small
\alpha
.
Assuming star-convexity, we bound this difference by using the fact that a copy of
(1-\alpha)\mathds{V}
can be mapped into
(1+\alpha)\mathds{V}
while preserving distance and volume (
Lemma

3.5
), which proves
Footnote

-0.1
.

Under a choice of
X
, with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|
polynomial in
(\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right),\delta^{-1},\varepsilon^{-1})
,
Footnote

-0.1
is an approximation-preserving reduction from
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
to
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
that is polynomial in
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
and produces a graph
G_{X}
of size polynomial in
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
.
In
Section

1.3
, we discuss how to choose the point set
X
with
|X|\in O((\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right))^{2})
in the case of cubic regions
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Applying

-0.1
allows us to use known algorithmic results on the resulting hard-core model to efficiently approximate
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
.
Our bound on the required number of points in this setting is asymptotically tight in
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
for general hard-constraint point processes, as we show in
Section

5.1
.

For cubic regions, we show that there is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation with
\delta=0
.
However, allowing for a small margin of volume error
\delta>0
in
Footnote

-0.1
results in interesting connections between the partition function of the hard-core model on random geometric graphs and the continuous partition function of the hard-constraint point process, as we discuss in
Section

1.5
.
Namely, we show that the hard-core partition function on such geometric random graphs concentrates around that of a hard-constraint point process.
In fact, this even holds without the assumption of star-convexity, required in
Footnote

-0.1
.

For the case of the hard-sphere model on cubic regions, the same discretization scheme was used before to obtain a randomized approximation algorithm
[
FGK+21
]
.
However, as this result requires the size of the graph to be super-exponential in
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
, the previous algorithm needs to utilize the succinct representation and structural information of the produced graph
G
, which was highly specific to the hard-sphere model.
Since our refined bound yields much smaller graphs, we are able to use the results on the hard-core model as out-of-the-box algorithms.
Besides the aforementioned algorithmic simplifications, our result extends the applicability to other hard-constraint point processes including the Widom–Rowlinson model.
Further, it allows for the first efficient deterministic approximation within the same parameter regimes for both, the hard-sphere model and the Widom–Rowlinson model.

1.3
Approximation algorithms via canonical discretization

We show how
Footnote

-0.1
can be used to obtain approximations for the partition functions of the hard-sphere model and the Widom–Rowlinson model.
To this end, we develop our result in the setting of general hard-constraint point processes and obtain the model-specific results as corollaries.
Although
Footnote

-0.1
applies to a variety of regions
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
,
we focus on cubic regions
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
with
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
for stating our algorithmic results, as such regions exhibit a straightforward way to construct point sets with small allocations.
We suggest the following choice, which we call
canonical discretization
.
The point set
X\subset\mathds{V}
consists of a
d
-dimensional grid of side length
n
, and the allocation
\Phi
maps each point
y=\big(y^{(i)}\big)_{i\in[d]}\in\mathds{V}
to the closest point in
x=\big(x^{(i)}\big)_{i\in[d]}\in X
such that, for all
i\in[d]
, it holds that
x^{(i)}\leq y^{(i)}
.
Since, for each
x\in X
, the set
\Phi^{-1}(x)
has the same volume, this results in a
0
-
\varepsilon
-allocation.
By
Footnote

-0.1
, in order to approximate the partition function of the hard-constraint point process
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
, it suffices to do so for the partition function of the hard-core model on
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
.

We first study the case where the considered hard-constraint point process only contains a single particle type (e.g., the hard-sphere model) or all particle types have the same fugacity (e.g., the Widom–Rowlinson model with uniform fugacities).
In this case, the hard-core representation is a uniform hard-core model, i.e., there is a constant
\lambda
such that, for each
v
, it holds that
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=\lambda
.
A selection of algorithmic results are applicable in this setting.
Usually, approximation algorithms for the partition function of the uniform hard-core model apply to instances
(G,\lambda)
where
G
has bounded maximum degree
\Delta
and
\lambda<\lambda_{\text{c}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta}}\right)=\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta-1}}\right)^{\Delta-1}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta-2}}\right)^{\Delta}}\approx\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\Delta}
.
On general graphs, this parameter regime is tight, as approximating the partition function for a hard-core model is known to be
NP
-hard for instances
(G,\lambda)
with
\lambda>\lambda_{\text{c}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta}}\right)

[
Sly10
,
GGS+14
]
.
We obtain our algorithms by bounding the maximum degree of
G_{X}
and using the known approximation algorithms for the hard-core model.
Namely, we use a Monte-Carlo Markov chain algorithm, for which polynomial running time was recently proven for all
\Delta
in a sequence of papers by
[
ALG20
,
CLV20
,
CLV21
,
CFY+21
]
.
Furthermore, using the deterministic approximation algorithm of
[
Wei06
]
with running time in
n^{\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\log\Delta}}}\right)}
, we obtain a deterministic algorithm with running time quasi-polynomial in the volume of

\mathds{V}

​​.
-0.1
-0.1

-0.1

We get the same result by combining the results of
[
PR19
]
with the method of
[
Bar16
]
.

In order to formally state our algorithmic results for the class of hard-constraint point processes, we introduce the
volume exclusion matrix

B\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}^{q\times q}
of a hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particles types.
For all
i,j\in[q]
, it holds that
B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)=\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)
, where
\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)
denotes a
d
-dimensional ball of radius
r
.
Intuitively, the entry
B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)
gives an upper bound on the volume of the region around a particle of type
i\in[q]
in which no particle of type
j\in[q]
can be placed.

Our main algorithmic result for general hard-constraint point processes is the following.

{restatable*}
theoremuniformapprox
Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types,
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, and let
\lambda
be a constant.
Let
B
be the corresponding volume exclusion matrix and denote its
L_{1}
-norm by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}
.
If

\lambda<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}},

then for all
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}\in(0,1]
, there is a randomized
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}
-approximation algorithm for
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\big({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}\big)
and a quasi-polynomial deterministic approximation algorithm for
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
with running time
\big(\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}\big)^{\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)/\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}\right)}}}\right)}
.

We derive our algorithmic results for the hard-core model and the Widom–Rowlinson model with uniform fugacities and radii from
Section

1.3
.
For the hard-sphere model, we obtain the following corollary.

{restatable*}
corollarymonoatomichs
Let
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Further, let
r\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
and
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Denote by
Z_{\text{HS}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda}}\right)
the hard-sphere partition function on
\mathds{V}
with particles of radius
r
and fugacity
\lambda
.
If

\lambda<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{2^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)},

then for all
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}\in(0,1]
, there is a randomized
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}
-approximation algorithm for
Z_{\text{HS}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda}}\right)
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\big({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}\big)
and a quasi-polynomial deterministic
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}
-approximation algorithm for
Z_{\text{HS}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda}}\right)
with running time
\big(\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}\big)^{\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)/\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}\right)}}}\right)}
.

Section

1.3
achieves the best known algorithmic bound in terms of
\lambda

[
FGK+21
,
MP21a
]
.
Furthermore, the quasi-polynomial deterministic approximation algorithm is the first efficient deterministic approximation for this parameter regime and partially answers an open question of
[
MP21a
]
.

For the Widom–Rowlinson model,
Section

1.3
yields the following rigorous approximation result.

{restatable*}
corollaryuniformwr
Let
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
,
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, let and
r\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
and
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Let
Z_{\text{WR}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda}}\right)
denote the Widom–Rowlinson partition function on
\mathds{V}
with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types, each of radius
r
and fugacity
\lambda
.
If

\lambda<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{(q-1)2^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)},

then for all
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}\in(0,1]
, there is a randomized
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}
-approximation algorithm for
Z_{\text{WR}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda}}\right)
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\big({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}\big)
and a quasi-polynomial deterministic
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}
-approximation algorithm for
Z_{\text{WR}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda}}\right)
with running time
\big(\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}\big)^{\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)/\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}\right)}}}\right)}
.

To the best of our knowledge, no other efficient approximation algorithm is known for this parameter regime.
In fact,
Section

1.3
also applies to the non-uniform Widom-Rowlinson model when replacing
\lambda
and
r
with the maximum fugacity and maximum radius among all particle types (see
Remark

6.1
).

For non-uniform hard-constraint point processes, i.e., each particle type has its own fugacity, the resulting instance of the hard-core model is also non-uniform.
There are some algorithmic results for the non-uniform hard-core model in the literature, but none of them is known to establish a tight condition.
To our knowledge, the broadest parameter regime for the non-uniform hard-core model is achieved by a randomized approximation algorithm
[
FGK+20
]
.
Applying this approximation algorithm to the instance
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
, we obtain the following algorithmic condition for the hard-constraint point process.

{restatable*}

theoremnonUniformApprox

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types and
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Further, let
B
be the corresponding volume exclusion matrix.
If there is a function
f\colon[q]\to\mathds{R}_{>0}
such that for all
i\in[q]
, it holds that

f(i)>\sum_{j\in[q]}B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)f(j)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right),

then for all
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}\in(0,1]
, there is a randomized
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}
-approximation algorithm for
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}},\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\max_{i\in[q]}f(i)}{\min_{i\in[q]}f(i)}}}\right)}}}\right)
.

For the non-uniform hard-core model, faster randomized algorithms
[
CGG+19
]
as well as deterministic algorithms
[
JKP19
]
have appeared in the literature.
These results are also applicable to our setting.
The stated results are chosen to yield the broadest parameter regime possible.

A particularly interesting corollary of
Section

1.3
is for the two-particle Widom–Rowlinson model.

{restatable*}
corollaryunbalancedWR

Let
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Further, let
r\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
,
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Denote by
Z_{\text{WR}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}}\right)
the Widom–Rowlinson partition function on
\mathds{V}
with
2
particle types, both with the same radius
r
but (possibly) different fugacities
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
.
If

\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}<\frac{1}{4^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)^{2}},

then for all
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}\in(0,1]
, there is a randomized
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}
-approximation algorithm for
Z_{\text{WR}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}}\right)
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\big({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}\big)
.

Note that this condition only requires the product of the fugacities of both particle types to be bounded.
Thus, one fugacity can be arbitrarily large as long as the other is sufficiently small, leading to an approximation whenever the model is sufficiently unbalanced.
One key observation to explain this behavior is that the resulting hard-core representation used in
Section

1.3
is a bipartite graph with different fugacities for each vertex partition.
Such hard-core models on unbalanced bipartite graphs are known to exhibit efficient approximations for broader parameter regimes, as shown by
[
CP20
]
.
However, their results do not appear to give feasible parameter regimes in our setting.

1.4
Sampling via random perturbations

So far, we only discussed approximation results for the partition functions of the hard-sphere model and the Widom–Rowlinson model, although, in discrete spin systems, approximation algorithms often go hand in hand with sampling algorithms
[
JVV86
]
.
Unfortunately, for continuous spin systems, a natural barrier is that outputting a sample, i.e., a tuple of points that represents a valid configuration, requires infinite floating-point precision.
Thus, assuming a discrete computational model, as is common in computer science, no (approximate) sampling algorithm with meaningful error bounds in terms of total-variation distance can be obtained.

Assuming a computational model that performs arithmetic operations of floating-point values with arbitrary precision and can uniformly sample a random floating-point number from an interval (for more details see
7.1
), we use our discretization
G_{X}
to recover an approximate sampler for
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}
.
Such an assumption is not satisfied by an ordinary computer.
However, for practical considerations, the floating-point precision of common discrete computational models might be seen as sufficient for applying our sampling approach.

Given a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation
\Phi
, our sampling algorithm (
Algorithm

1
) first samples an independent set
I
from the Gibbs distribution of the hard-core model on
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
, using a known Markov chain method (see, e.g.,
[
ALG20
,
CLV20
,
CFY+21
]
).
Recall that each vertex
v_{x}^{(i)}
of
G_{X}
corresponds to a point
x\in X
of a particle of type
i
.
Then, for each vertex
v_{x}^{(i)}
in
I
, our sampler chooses a position in
\Phi^{-1}(x)
uniformly at random and places a particle of type
i
at this position.
This way, we obtain the following sampling analogue of
Section

1.3
.

{restatable*}
theoremuniformSampling

Suppose a computational model that satisfies
7.1
.
Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types,
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, and let
\lambda
be a constant.
Let
B
be the corresponding volume exclusion matrix and denote its
L_{1}
-norm by
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}
.
If

\lambda<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}},

then for all
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}\in(0,1]
, there is an
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
-approximate sampler for
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\big({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}}\big)
.

Our sampling procedure does not always result in a valid configuration for the hard-constraint point process.
However, by bounding the total-variation distance between the two distributions,
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}
and the distribution of our sampler, we bound the probability of the sampler returning an invalid configuration by some small constant.
In this case, we reject this configuration and repeat the sampling procedure.

Observe that the sampling analogues of
Sections

1.3
and
1.3
immediately follow from the above theorem.
With
Algorithm

1
, we also obtain the following theorem for the non-uniform case.

{restatable*}
theoremnonUniformSampling

Suppose a computational model that satisfies
7.1
.
Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types and
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
,
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Further, let
B
be the corresponding volume exclusion matrix.
If there is a function
f\colon[q]\to\mathds{R}_{>0}
such that for all
i\in[q]
,

f(i)>\sum_{j\in[q]}B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)f(j)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right),

then for all
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}\in(0,1]
, there is an
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
-approximate sampler for
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}}}\right)
and
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\max_{i\in[q]}f(i)}{\min_{i\in[q]}f(i)}}}\right)}}}\right)
.

As with the uniform case, the sampling analogue of
Section

1.3
follows from the above theorem.

1.5
Concentration of random discretizations

As seen in
Sections

1.3
and
1.4
, our algorithmic results use a
0
-
\varepsilon
-allocation.
However,
Footnote

-0.1
allows for a more general set of allocations, where
\delta>0
.
Using this generality, we show sufficient conditions for the hard-core partition function of a discretization based on a uniformly random set of points
X
to concentrate around a the partition function of the respective hard-contraint point process.
Central to this condition is the notion of a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning.

Definition 1.2
(
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning).

Let
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
be bounded and measurable and assume
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)>0
.
For
\delta\in(0,1]
and
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, we say that a finite partitioning
(\mathds{V}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
of
\mathds{V}
is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning
of size
m
if and only if for all
k\in[m]
it holds that

1.

\mathds{V}_{k}
is measurable and

\delta\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{m}\leq\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{k}}}\right)

2.

for all
x,y\in\mathds{V}_{k}
it holds that

d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)\leq\varepsilon.

Our main result in this direction essentially says that, for star-convex regions
\mathds{V}
, the existence of a polynomial size
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning implies strong concentration of hard-core partition functions for random discretizations of polynomial size.

{restatable*}

corollaryrandomdiscretizationconcentration

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types, and assume
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
is star-convex.
Further, assume there is some
\delta\in(0,1]
such that for all
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
there is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning of
\mathds{V}
of size
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon}}}}\right)
.
For all
\varepsilon_{\text{D}}\in(0,1]
and
p\in(0,1]
there is some
n_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}},p}\in\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}p}}}\right)}}}\right)
such that for all
n\geq n_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}},p}
it holds for
X\subset\mathds{V}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|=n
uniformly at random that

\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)

with probability at least
1-p
.

An immediate consequence of
Section

1.5
is that, for uniform random geometric graphs on
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
with edge connection threshold
2r
and appropriately chosen fugacities, the hard-core partition function concentrates with increasing number of vertices around the partition function of a hard-sphere model with particle radius
r
on
\mathds{V}
.
There are ongoing efforts to prove concentration of different properties of geometric random graphs
[
M“”ul08
,
Bac16
,
BR18
]
.
To the best of our knowledge, our result is the first such concentration bound for hard-core partition functions.
Thus, we believe this connection to be interesting in its own right.

As the proof of
Section

1.5
uses
Footnote

-0.1
, it inherits the assumption that
\mathds{V}
is star-convex.
However, without assuming star-convexity, a slightly weaker concentration holds.

{restatable*}
theoremconcentrationnonstarconvex

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types.
Further, assume there is some
\delta\in(0,1]
such that for all
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
there is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning of
\mathds{V}
of size
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon}}}}\right)
.
For all
\varepsilon_{\text{D}}\in(0,1]
and
p\in(0,1]
there is some
n_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}},p}\in\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}p}}}}\right)
such that for all
n\geq n_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}},p}
it holds for
X\subset\mathds{V}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|=n
uniformly at random that

\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)

with probability at least
1-p
.

The main idea behind the proof of
Section

1.5
is that, even without star-convexity, it still holds that
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
is not much smaller than
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
if there is a suitable allocation for
X
.
It follows that, given
\mathds{V}
has a suitable partitioning,
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
concentrates from below.
We then establish a simple lemma that derives concentration from above, given sufficiently strong concentration from below for non-negative random variables.
We believe this tool to be of independent interest.

{restatable*}

lemmamodifiedmarkov

Let
X
be a non-negative random variable with finite expectation.
If there are
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
and
\delta\in[0,1]
such that
\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X<(1-\varepsilon)\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X}}}\right]}}}\right]\leq\delta
, then it holds that
\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X\geq(1+c\varepsilon)\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{X}}}\right]}}}\right]\leq\frac{1}{c+1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\delta(1-\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}}}\right)
for all
c\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.

1.6
Discussion and future directions

Our algorithmic results for the Widom–Rowlinson model and the hard-sphere model are centered around discretizations based on the hard-core model.
For the hard-sphere model, similar algorithmic results have recently been obtained without discretization by directly applying a Markov chain Monte Carlo method to the continuous problem, assuming a continuous model of computation
[
MP21a
]
.
However, this approach is inherently probabilistic, whereas we have shown that discretization also yields efficient deterministic algorithms for the same parameter regime.
It would be interesting to see how to reproduce such results directly using the continuous model.
One approach could be to approximate the logarithm of the partition function instead, for example by using the cluster expansion.
This has successfully been done in the setting of discrete models
[
PR17
,
HPR19
]
.
However, in contrast to the discrete setting, we are not aware of any method to compute the coefficients of the expansion efficiently for continuous problems, which would be required for algorithmic application.

A major question for future research is if the parameter bounds for approximation that are presented in this paper can be improved further.
An obvious idea would be to use more detailed insights about the properties of the graphs that result from the discretization.
A candidate for that could be the connective constant, which was used before to improve algorithmic results for the hard-core model on certain graph classes
[
SSS+17
]
.
However, arguments were made that for the canonical discretization of the hard-sphere model, the connective constant is asymptotically equivalent to the maximum degree of the graph (see
[
Pen94
]
and
[
FGK+21
, Section 1.4]
).
On the other hand,
[
MP21
]
recently introduced the notion of a potential-weighted connective constant in the continuous setting to increase the regime of uniqueness of the Gibbs measure for the hard-sphere model in low dimensions by using structural properties of Euclidean space.
This raises the question if the potential-weighted connective constant can be translated to the discrete setting, leading to a similar improvement for approximating the partition function for low dimensions.

Another interesting algorithmic question is whether the quasi-polynomial running time of the deterministic approximation algorithm can be improved to a polynomial.
The two techniques yielding deterministic algorithms for the hard-core model have a running time of
n^{\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\log\Delta}}}\right)}
for graphs of maximum degree
\Delta
, which corresponds in our setting to the quasi-polynomial running time.
In the correlation decay method of Weitz
[
Wei06
]
, this running time comes from computing the self-avoiding walk tree of
G
.
In Barvinok’s interpolation method, this comes from enumerating induced connected subgraphs
[
PR17
]
.
Constructing a deterministic algorithm for general graphs of maximum degree
\Delta
and better running-time dependency on
\Delta
, if possible, seems to require significant conceptual insight.
However, one could hope that the symmetric structure of
G_{X}
, produced by our discretization, leads to faster running times for the computational tasks used in any of these two algorithmic techniques.

Finally, it is worth pointing out that there are a variety of models in statistical physics that do not fit into the framework we considered.
For instance, in
[
MP21a
]
the problem of obtaining a randomized approximating for the partition function of single type Gibbs point processes with general repulsive potentials is considered.
It would be interesting to see to what extend discretization based approaches can also be applied in this setting.

2
Preliminaries

Throughout the paper, denote by
\mathds{N}
the set of all non-negative integers, including
0
and write
\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
for the set
\mathds{N}\setminus\{0\}
.
For all
n\in
denote by
[n]
the set
[1,n]\cap\mathds{N}=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.
Furthermore, we use the convention that
\inf\emptyset=\infty
.

Let
S
be some set and
f\colon S\to\mathds{R}
.
For any
\alpha\in\mathds{R}
we write
\alpha f
for the function
s\mapsto\alpha f(s)
for all
s\in S
.
Within this work, we occasionally encounter functions whose domain is the empty set.
Recall that, following the definition of functions as subsets of the Cartesian product, there is exactly one function
f\colon\emptyset\to S
for all sets
S
.

For any subset of
d
-dimensional Euclidean space
A\subseteq\mathds{R}^{d}
we write
\alpha A
for the set
\{\alpha x\mid x\in A\}
.
Let
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)
denote the Euclidean distance between points
x,y\in\mathds{R}^{d}
.
For
x\in\mathds{R}^{d}
and
r\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, we write
\mathds{B}_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)
for a the open ball of radius
r
around
x
, formally
\mathds{B}_{x}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)=\{y\in\mathds{R}^{d}\mid d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)<r\}
.
When the center
x
of the ball is not important, we simply omit it and write
\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)
instead.
Further, we denote the Lebesgue measure on
\mathds{R}^{d}
by
\nu^{d}
.
We extend this notation by writing
\nu^{d\times k}
for the product of
k
Lebesgue measures, each on
\mathds{R}^{d}
.
Whenever
d
and
k
are clear from the context, we simply write
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\cdot}}\right)
to simplify notation.
This is, for all measurable sets
A\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
we write
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A}}\right)=\nu^{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(A}}\right)
, and for all measurable sets
B\subset\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{R}^{d}}}\right)^{k}
we write
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B}}\right)=\nu^{d\times k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(B}}\right)
.
Finally, a set
A\subseteq\mathds{R}^{d}
is called
star-convex
if there is a point
x\in A
such that for all
y\in A
and all
\alpha\in[0,1]
it holds that
\alpha x+(1-\alpha)y\in A
.
We say that any such point
x\in A
is a center of
A
.

2.1
Notions of approximation

For
x\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
and some
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
we call
x^{\prime}\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
an
\varepsilon
-approximation for
x
if
\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}x\leq x^{\prime}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon}x
.
Further, we call a real-valued random variable
X
a
randomized
\varepsilon
-approximation for
x
if

\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon}x\leq X\leq\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon}x}}}\right]\geq 3/4
.

For approximate sampling we use the following formal notion.
Let
\xi
be a probability distribution on a state space
\Omega
.
For
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, we say that a distribution
\xi^{\prime}
on
\Omega
is an
\varepsilon
-approximation of
\xi
if
d_{\text{TV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\xi,\xi^{\prime}}}\right)\leq\varepsilon
, where
d_{\text{TV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\cdot,\cdot}}\right)
denotes the total-variation distance.
Further, we say that we can
\varepsilon
-approximately sample from
\xi
if we can sample from any distribution
\xi^{\prime}
such that
\xi^{\prime}
is an
\varepsilon
-approximation of
\xi
.

2.2
The hard-core model

We recall the definition of the hard-core model from the introduction.
For an undirected graph
G=(V,E)
the multivariate hard-core model is defined as a tuple
(G,\lambda)
where
\lambda\colon V\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Let
\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
denote the set of all independent sets in
G
.
We assign each independent set
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
a weight of
\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
.
The multivariate hard-core partition function is now defined as the sum of weights of all independent sets.
Further, the Gibbs distribution of the multivariate hard-core model assigns each independent set a probability proportional to its weight.
Formally, that is

Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\text{ and }\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(I}}\right)=\frac{\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}{Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)}\text{ for all $I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)$.}

If
\lambda
is a constant, we call the hard-core model univariate (or uniform).
Note that in most of the existing literature the term hard-core model refers to the univariate version.
Further, the multivariate case appears sometimes as polymer models in the algorithmic context.
However, in fact there are some conceptual differences between both terms, such as that polymer models are used to encode other computational problems and tend to be exponentially large in the original problem instance, which means that no explicit representation of the polymer graph is assumed.
We stick to this conceptual difference, and thus, we use the term hard-core model.
Unless we explicitly state that any of our statements is limited to the univariate case, we will use the term hard-core model for the multivariate version.
Further, whenever we deal with the univariate case, we will abuse notation and simply write
\lambda
as a constant instead of
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
.
Note that this simplifies the weight of an independent
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
to
\lambda^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|I}}\right|}
, which directly carries over to the definitions of the partition function and the Gibbs distribution.

The following paragraphs summarize known algorithmic results for approximate sampling from the Gibbs distribution and approximating the partition function.
For obtaining a deterministic approximation of the partition function, two main algorithmic approaches are known, namely the computational tree method, introduces by
[
Wei06
]
, and the polynomial interpolation method of
[
PR17
,
Bar16
]
.
To get a randomized approximation, usually a Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithm based on self-reducibility is used.
For this approach, it is necessary to approximately sample from the Gibbs distribution.
This is often done via
Glauber dynamics
, which is an ergodic Markov chain with state space
\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
and stationary distribution
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)}
.
In the multivariate setting, the update rule of this Markov chain works as follows.
Let
I_{t}
be the state of the chain at time
t
.
The configuration at time
t+1
is obtained by first choosing a vertex
v\in V
uniformly at random.
With probability
\frac{1}{1+\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}
, we set
I_{t+1}=I_{t}\setminus\{v\}
(note that nothing changes if
v\notin I_{t}
).
Otherwise, with probability
\frac{\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}{1+\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}
, we set
I_{t+1}=I_{t}\cup\{v\}
if
I_{t}\cup\{v\}\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
.

Univariate approximation:

For the univariate hard-core model, the ability to efficiently approximate the partition function is closely related to the maximum degree of the graph.
For a graph of maximum degree
\Delta\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 2}
, the tree threshold is defined as

\lambda_{\text{c}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta}}\right)=\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta-1}}\right)^{\Delta-1}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta-2}}\right)^{\Delta}}.

The following theorems show some of the algorithmic results that are known below this threshold.
The first statement deals with approximately sampling from the Gibbs distribution of the univariate hard-core model via Glauber dynamics.

Theorem 2.1
(
[
CFY+21
, Theorem
1.3
]
).

Let
G=(V,E)
be an undirected graph with maximum vertex degree bounded by
\Delta\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 2}
.
For all
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
with
\lambda<\lambda_{\text{c}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta}}\right)

the Glauber dynamics on the univariate hard-core model
(G,\lambda)
are mixing in time
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|V}}\right|}}}\right)
, and for all
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}\in(0,1]
we can
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
-approximately sample from the Gibbs measure
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)}
in time
\mathrm{O}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({n\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(n\ln(\Delta)+\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)
.

It is well known that, based on such a sampler, a Markov chain Monte Carlo approximation for the partition function of the hard-core model can be obtained using self-reducibility.
Thus,
Theorem

2.1
leads to the following approximation result.

Theorem 2.2
.

Let
G=(V,E)
be an undirected graph with maximum vertex degree bounded by
\Delta\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 2}
and let
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
with
\lambda<\lambda_{\text{c}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta}}\right)
.
For all
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}\in(0,1]
there is a randomized
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}
-approximation algorithm for the univariate hard-core partition function
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)
with running time
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{n}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}}}\right)
.

Besides this randomized algorithm, the following result can be obtained deterministically.

Theorem 2.3
(
[
Wei06
, Theorem
2.7
]
).

Let
G=(V,E)
be an undirected graph with maximum vertex degree bounded by
\Delta\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 2}
and let
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
with
\lambda<\lambda_{\text{c}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta}}\right)
.
For all
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}\in(0,1]
there is a deterministic
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}
-approximation algorithm for the univariate hard-core partition function
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)
with running time
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|V}}\right|}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}}\right)^{\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\ln(\Delta)}}}\right)}
.

Note that
Theorem

2.2
is based on the computational tree method by
[
Wei06
]
.
A similar result can be obtained by combining the zero-freeness result by
[
PR19
]
and the polynomial interpolation method as proposed by
[
PR17
]
.

Remark 2.4
.

In fact,
Theorems

2.1
,
2.2
and
2.3
can also be applied in the non-uniform setting when the condition is replaced by

\lambda_{\max}<\lambda_{\text{c}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta}}\right),

where
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{v\in V}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
.

Multivariate approximation:

One of the least restrictive conditions that appeared in the literature for efficient approximation of multivariate hard-core partition functions is the clique dynamics condition, introduced by
[
FGK+20
]
.
Initially, the condition was used for proving rapid mixing of clique dynamics for abstract polymer models.
However Glauber dynamics can, in fact, be seen as a special case of clique dynamics for cliques of size
1
.
The result for Glauber dynamics is summarized in the following statement.

Theorem 2.5
(
[
FGK+20
, Theorem
2
]
).

Let
(G,\lambda)
be a multivariate hard-core model.
If there is a function
f\colon V\to\mathds{R}_{>0}
such that for all
v\in V
it holds that

f(v)\geq\sum_{w\in N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}f(w)\frac{\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)}{1+\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)},

where
N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
denotes the neighborhood of
v
in
G
, then the Glauber dynamics on
(G,\lambda)
are mixing in time
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|V}}\right|,\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\max_{v\in V}f(v)}{\min_{v\in V}f(v)}}}\right)}}}\right)
.
Further, for all
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}\in(0,1]
we can sample
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
-approximately from the Gibbs distribution
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)}
in time
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|V}}\right|}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}},\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\max_{v\in V}f(v)}{\min_{v\in V}f(v)}}}\right)}}}\right)
.

Further, the following approximation result is obtained via a Markov chain Monte Carlo approach and
Theorem

2.5
.

Theorem 2.6
(
[
FGK+20
, Theorem
3
]
).

Let
(G,\lambda)
be a multivariate hard-core model.
If there is a function
f\colon V\to\mathds{R}_{>0}
such that for all
v\in V
it holds that

f(v)\geq\sum_{w\in N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}f(w)\frac{\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)}{1+\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)},

then for all
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}\in(0,1]
there is a randomized
\varepsilon_{\text{A}}
-approximation algorithm for the partition function
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|V}}\right|}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}},\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\max_{v\in V}f(v)}{\min_{v\in V}f(v)}}}\right)}}}\right)
.

3
Point processes with general hard constraints

We recall the definition of a hard-constraint process from the introduction and give some alternative forms of the partition function.
Let
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
be bounded and measurable, and let
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
.
The model represents the distribution of particles of
q
types, labeled by elements in
[q]\coloneqq[1,q]\cap\mathds{N}
, on
\mathds{V}
.
Particles of the same types are assumed to be indistinguishable.
Let
\lambda\colon[q]\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
be a function that equips each particle type with a fugacity.
For each particle type
i\in[q]
, we assume that the positions of particles are distributed according to a (labeled) Poisson point process of intensity
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
on
\mathds{V}
.
To add the constraints to the model, let
R\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}^{q\times q}
be a symmetric
q\times q
matrix, called the
interaction matrix
.
We condition the mixture of point processes by rejecting all configurations that contain particles at positions
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathds{V}
with corresponding particle types
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in[q]
whenever
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},x_{2}}}\right)<R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}}\right)
.
That is, the entries of
R
determine the minimum distance that particles of the respective types can have.
Especially note that
R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}}}\right)=0
means that the particle types
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in[q]
are not subjected to any pairwise constraints.

For any instance
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
of a hard-constraint point process that satisfies the requirements above, this description characterizes a
Gibbs distribution
as follows.
For all
k\in\mathds{N}
and all type assignments
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
let
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\colon\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{R}^{d}}}\right)^{k}\to\{0,1\}
be the function that indicates for a tuple of particles positions
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{R}^{d}}}\right)^{k}
if it forms a valid configuration, assuming that for each
i\in[k]
the particle at
x_{i}
is of type
\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
.
Formally, this is

D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]\colon\\
i<j\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i},x_{j}}}\right)\geq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}}\right)}}\right\}},

where
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=1
for the case that
k=0
.
For each tuple
(\bm{x},\tau)
as above, the Gibbs distribution of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
is defined via the probability density

\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)=\frac{\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}{Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)},

where the normalizing constant
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
is called the
partition function
.
This is

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)=1+\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

(1)

The main goal of this paper is to investigate condition for an efficient (randomized) approximation of
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
.
Occasionally it will be convenient to include the case
k=0
in the sum in
equation

1
.
To this end, we use the convention
\int_{\mathds{V}^{0}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times 0}=1
to rewrite
equation

1
as

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

We can further simplify this notation by defining a weight

w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)=\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)

for each
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathds{V}^{k}
and
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
.
Using this weight function, we can rewrite the Gibbs density and the partition function slightly more compact as

\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)=\frac{w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}{Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\text{ and }Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

For formalizing our conditions for efficient approximation of the partition function of a hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
it will come in handy to introduce the
volume exclusion matrix

B\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}^{q\times q}
the model.
It is defined by the entries
B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)=\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)
for all
i,j\in[q]
.
Intuitively speaking, this means that the entry
B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)
gives an upper bound on the volume of the region around a point
x\in\mathds{V}
in which no particle of type
j\in[q]
can be placed, given we place a particle of type
i\in[q]
at
x
.

For our algorithmic results, we investigate asymptotic behavior in the volume
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
, while we assume the parameters
q
,
d
, and the entries of
R
as well as the range of
\lambda
to be constant.

3.1
Scaling, bounds and monotonicity

We start by collecting some useful properties of the partition function of general hard-constraint point processes, which will come in handy when bounding the speed of convergence of our discretization.
Our first lemma gives a trivial upper bound on the partition function.

Lemma 3.1
.

For every hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types it holds that

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}.

Proof.

Fix some
k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 0}
and observe that for any
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
it holds that

\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}\leq\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}^{k}}}\right)=\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{k}.

Now, fix some
(k_{1},\dots,k_{q})\in\mathds{N}^{q}
such that
\sum_{i\in[q]}k_{i}=k
.
Observe that there are exactly
\frac{k!}{\prod_{i\in[q]}k_{i}!}
type assignments
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
such that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\tau^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right|=k_{i}
for all
i\in[q]
.
Further, for each such type assignment, it holds that

\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)=\prod_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{k_{i}}.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k_{1},\dots,k_{q})\in\mathds{N}^{q}\colon\\
k_{1}+\dots+k_{q}=k\end{subarray}}\prod_{i\in[q]}\frac{\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{k_{i}}}{k_{i}!}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{k}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)^{k}}{k!},

where the last equality is obtained via the multinomial theorem.
The claim follows from the Taylor expansion of the exponential function around
0
.
∎

Our next observation is concerned with the monotonicity of the partition function of hard-constraint point processes with respect to
\mathds{V}
,
R
and
\lambda
.

Observation 3.2
.

Fix some
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
.
Let
\mathds{V},\mathds{V}_{1},\mathds{V}_{2}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
, let
R,R_{1},R_{2}\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}^{q\times q}
be symmetric and let
\lambda\colon[q]\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0},\lambda_{1}\colon[q]\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0},\lambda_{2}\colon[q]\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
It holds that:

1.

If
\mathds{V}_{1}\subseteq\mathds{V}_{2}
then
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{1},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{2},R,\lambda}}\right)
.

2.

If
R_{1}(i,j)\leq R_{2}(i,j)
for all
i,j\in[q]
then
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R_{1},\lambda}}\right)\geq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R_{2},\lambda}}\right)
.

3.

If
\lambda_{1}(i)\leq\lambda_{2}(i)
for all
i\in[q]
then
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda_{1}}}\right)\leq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda_{2}}}\right)
.

It is a folklore that the partition function the hard-sphere model and the Widom–Rowlinson model are log-submodular in the considered region.
The following lemma formalizes this idea for general hard-constraint point processes.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\mathds{V}_{1}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
and
\mathds{V}_{2}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
be bounded and measurable, and let
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
.
For symmetric
R\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}^{q\times q}
and all functions
\lambda\colon[q]\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
it holds that

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{1}\cup\mathds{V}_{2},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{1},R,\lambda}}\right)\cdot Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{2},R,\lambda}}\right)

Proof.

First, note that it is sufficient to prove the claim for disjoint sets
\mathds{V}_{1}
and
\mathds{V}_{2}
.
For non-disjoint
\mathds{V}_{1},\mathds{V}_{2}
the claim then follows from partitioning
\mathds{V}_{1}\cup\mathds{V}_{2}
into the disjoint sets
\mathds{V}_{1}
and
\mathds{V}_{2}\setminus\mathds{V}_{1}
, and by noting that
\mathds{V}_{2}\setminus\mathds{V}_{1}\subseteq\mathds{V}_{2}
, which by
3.2
implies
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{2}\setminus\mathds{V}_{1},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{2},R,\lambda}}\right)
.

Thus, assume
\mathds{V}_{1}\cap\mathds{V}_{2}=\emptyset
and set
\mathds{V}=\mathds{V}_{1}\cup\mathds{V}_{2}
.
Fix some
k\in\mathds{N}
.
For
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathds{V}^{k}
, let
k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)
denote the number from
\mathds{V}_{1}
.
Formally, this is
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\{i\in[k]\mid x_{i}\in\mathds{V}_{1}\}}}\right|
.
As a first step, we rewrite the partition function on
\mathds{V}
as

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathds{N}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathds{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=k_{1}}}\right\}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathds{N}}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathds{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=k_{1}}}\right\}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

Fix some
k\in\mathds{N}
and let
\pi\colon[k]\to[k]
be a bijective (i.e.,
\pi
a permutation of
[k]
).
For every type assignment
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
we write
\pi\circ\tau
