for the composition of both functions.
That is, for all
i\in[k]
we have
(\pi\circ\tau)(i)=\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)
.
Further, for all
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathds{V}^{k}
we abuse notation and write
\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=(x_{\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)},\dots,x_{\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k}}\right)})
.
Observe that for all permutations
\pi\colon[k]\to[k]
it holds that

D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=D_{\pi\circ\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right).

Next, observe that for all
\bm{x}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
there is exactly one permutation
\pi_{\bm{x}}\colon[k]\to[k]
such that
\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\in\mathds{V}_{1}^{k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}\times\mathds{V}_{2}^{k-k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}
and the order among particle centers from
\mathds{V}_{1}
and from
\mathds{V}_{2}
is preserved.
Formally, that means
\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\in\mathds{V}_{1}^{k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}\times\mathds{V}_{2}^{k-k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}
, for all
i,j\in[k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)]
it holds that
\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)<\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)
if and only if
i<j
, and for all
i,j\in[k]\setminus[k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)]
it also holds that
\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)<\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)
if and only if
i<j
.
We can now rewrite

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\pi_{\bm{x}}\circ\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\pi_{\bm{x}}\circ\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right),

where the last equality is due to the fact that applying the permutation
\pi_{\bm{x}}
does only change the order of the finite product.

For the next step, note that for every fixed permutation
\pi_{\bm{x}}
and every fixed type assignment
\tau
there is exactly one type assignment
\tau^{\prime}
such that
\tau^{\prime}=(\pi_{\bm{x}}\circ\tau)
.
This means, applying
\pi_{\bm{x}}
to
\tau
does only change the order of summation.
We obtain

\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right).

To proceed, we need to introduce an additional bit of notation.
Let
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathds{N}
, and let
\tau_{1}\colon[k_{1}]\to[q]
and
\tau_{2}\colon[k_{2}]\to[q]
.
We write
\tau_{1}\oplus\tau_{2}
to denote the function
(\tau_{1}\oplus\tau_{2})\colon[k_{1}+k_{2}]\to[q]
such that for all
i\in[k_{1}+k_{2}]
we have

(\tau_{1}\oplus\tau_{2})(i)=\begin{cases}\tau_{1}(i)\text{ if }i\leq k_{1}\\
\tau_{2}(i-k_{1})\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Note that for each type assignment
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
and every fixed
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathds{N}
such that
k_{1}+k_{2}=k
, there is exactly one pair of assignments
\tau_{1}\colon[k_{1}]\to[q]
and
\tau_{2}\colon[k_{2}]\to[q]
such that
\tau=(\tau_{1}\oplus\tau_{2})
.
Further, for each such decomposition and all
(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathds{V}^{k}
it holds that

D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)=D_{\tau_{1}\oplus\tau_{2}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)\leq D_{\tau_{1}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k_{1}}}}\right)\cdot D_{\tau_{2}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{k_{1}+1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right).

For
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})\in\mathds{V}^{k}
, let
\varrho_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=(x_{\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)},\dots,x_{\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)})
, and let
\varrho_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=(x_{\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)+1}}\right)},\dots,x_{\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(k}}\right)})
.
This is,
\varrho_{1}
and
\varrho_{2}
project
\bm{x}
to those component that come from
\mathds{V}_{1}
and
\mathds{V}_{2}
respectively while preserving the order among them.
We obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\pi_{\bm{x}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\tau_{1}\colon[k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1}(i)}}\right)}}\right)\sum_{\tau_{2}\colon[k-k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k-k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{2}(i)}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau_{1}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varrho_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)\cdot D_{\tau_{2}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varrho_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)

and by substituting this into the definition of the partition function we get

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{k_{1}\in[k]}\sum_{\tau_{1}\colon[k_{1}]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k_{1}]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1}(i)}}\right)}}\right)\sum_{\tau_{2}\colon[k-k_{1}]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k-k_{1}]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{2}(i)}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathds{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{k_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=k_{1}}}\right\}}D_{\tau_{1}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varrho_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)\cdot D_{\tau_{2}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\varrho_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

Now, note that for all
k\in\mathds{N}
and
k_{1}\in[k]
and for all
\bm{y}\in\mathds{V}_{1}^{k_{1}}
and
\bm{z}\in\mathds{V}_{2}^{k-k_{1}}
it holds that there are exactly
\frac{k!}{k_{1}!(k-k_{1})!}
configurations
\bm{x}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
such that
\varrho_{1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=\bm{y}
and
\varrho_{2}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=\bm{z}
.
Thus, it holds that

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{k_{1}\in[k]}\frac{1}{k_{1}!}\sum_{\tau_{1}\colon[k_{1}]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k_{1}]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1}(i)}}\right)}}\right)\frac{1}{(k-k_{1})!}\sum_{\tau_{2}\colon[k-k_{1}]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k-k_{1}]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{2}(i)}}\right)}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt\cdot\int_{\mathds{V}_{1}^{k_{1}}}\int_{\mathds{V}_{2}^{k-k_{1}}}D_{\tau_{1}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{y}}}\right)\cdot D_{\tau_{2}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{z}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times(k-k_{1})}\text{d}\nu^{d\times k_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{k_{1}\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{k_{1}!}\sum_{\tau_{1}\colon[k_{1}]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k_{1}]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1}(i)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}_{1}^{k_{1}}}D_{\tau_{1}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{y}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k_{1}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{(k-k_{1})!}\sum_{\tau_{2}\colon[k-k_{1}]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k-k_{1}]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{2}(i)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}_{2}^{k-k_{1}}}D_{\tau_{2}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{z}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times(k-k_{1})}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathds{N}}\frac{1}{k_{1}!}\sum_{\tau_{1}\colon[k_{1}]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k_{1}]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{1}(i)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}_{1}^{k_{1}}}D_{\tau_{1}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{y}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k_{1}}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{k_{2}\in\mathds{N}}\frac{1}{k_{2}!}\sum_{\tau_{2}\colon[k_{2}]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k_{2}]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau_{2}(i)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}_{2}^{k_{2}}}D_{\tau_{2}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{z}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k_{2}}}}\right)

\displaystyle=Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{1},R,\lambda}}\right)\cdot Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{2},R,\lambda}}\right),

which concludes the proof.
∎

To provide a sufficient error bound for our discretization, it will be helpful to translate between scaling the particle radii
R
, the fugacities
\lambda
and the space itself
\mathds{V}
.
In Euclidean space, this can be done as stated in the following lemma.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types in
d\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
dimensions.
For all
\alpha\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
it holds that

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},\frac{1}{\alpha}R,\lambda}}\right)=Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha\mathds{V},R,\frac{1}{\alpha^{d}}\lambda}}\right).

Proof.

The key observation to prove this claim is that for all pairs of points
x,y\in\mathds{V}
and types
i,j\in[q]
it holds that
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{\alpha}R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)
if and only if
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha x,\alpha y}}\right)\geq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)
.
Thus, we get for all
k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
and all type assignments
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha x_{1},\dots,\alpha x_{k}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha^{dk}}\int_{(\alpha\mathds{V})^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{k}}}\right)\,\mathrm{d}\nu^{d\times k},

where the additional factor of
\frac{1}{\alpha^{dk}}
compensates for the scaling of the Lebesgue measure under linear transformation.

Substituting this back into the definition of the partition function we obtain

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},\frac{1}{\alpha}R,\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\frac{1}{\alpha^{dk}}\int_{(\alpha\mathds{V})^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle=1+\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\frac{\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}{\alpha^{d}}}}\right)\int_{(\alpha\mathds{V})^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle=Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\alpha\mathds{V},R,\frac{1}{\alpha^{d}}\lambda}}\right),

which proves the claim.
∎

Based on
Lemmas

3.1
and
3.2
and
Lemmas

3.3
and
3.4
we can now prove the following bound on the difference of partition functions on star-convex regions
\mathds{V}
with differently scaled particle interaction matrices
R
, which will be the very core of our convergence result.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,1}}\right]
, and let
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
be bounded, measurable and star-convex.
For any hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types it holds that

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1-\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)-Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1+\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\alpha}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

Proof.

Let
x_{0}\in\mathds{V}
denote the center of
\mathds{V}
.
Note that the partition function is invariant under translation.
Thus, we can assume that
x_{0}=0
.
Note that this especially implies for all
\beta_{1}\in[0,1]
and
\beta_{2}\in[1,\infty)
that
\beta_{1}\mathds{V}\subseteq\mathds{V}\subseteq\beta_{2}\mathds{V}
.

We proceed by rewriting the difference based on
Lemma

3.4
as

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1-\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle-Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1+\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle=Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)-Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1+\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right).

(2)

Using the fact that
\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V}\subseteq\mathds{V}\subseteq\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V}
and
Lemma

3.3
we obtain

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)\leq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V}\setminus\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right).

Because
(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\leq(1+\alpha)^{d}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
for all
i\in[q]
we can further bound this by
3.2
to get

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)\leq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V}\setminus\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1+\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right).

(3)

Combining
equations

2
and
3
yields

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1-\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)-

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1+\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V}\setminus\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1+\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt-Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1+\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V}\setminus\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1+\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right).

(4)

Using
Lemma

3.1
and the facts that
\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V}\subseteq\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V}
,
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V}}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}}\right)^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
and
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V}}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}}\right)^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
we further get

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V}\setminus\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{(1-\alpha)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V}\setminus\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{(1-\alpha)^{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}-\frac{1}{1+\alpha}}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}.

Further, for
0\leq\alpha
, it holds that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}}}\right)^{d}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-(1-2\alpha)}}\right)^{d}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\alpha}}\right)^{d}.

Thus, we obtain

\displaystyle Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathds{V}\setminus\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1-\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\alpha}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}.

(5)

Substituting
equation

5
into
equation

4
now gives us

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1-\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)-Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1+\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\alpha}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1+\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right).

Finally, because
Lemma

3.4
,
3.2
and the fact that
(1+\alpha)R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\geq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)
for all
i,j\in[q]
we have

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\mathds{V},R,(1+\alpha)^{d}\lambda}}\right)=Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1+\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

This gives us

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1-\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)-Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},(1+\alpha)R,\lambda}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\alpha}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right),

and concludes the proof.
∎

4
Discretization and convergence

We recall the construction of the graph
G_{X}
from the introduction.
Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be an instance of a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types.
For any finite and non-empty set of points
X\subseteq\mathds{V}
, we construct an undirected graph
G_{X}=(V_{X},E_{X})
and a function
\lambda_{X}\colon V_{X}\to\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
as follows:

•

For each point
x\in X
and each type
i\in[q]
, we construct a vertex
v_{x}^{(i)}
.
Further, for each
i\in[q]
, we set
V_{X}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{v_{x}^{(i)}\ \middle|\ x\in X}}\right\}
, and we set
V_{X}=\bigcup_{i\in[q]}V_{X}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}
.

•

For each
i,j\in[q]
and
x,y\in X
, we connect
v_{x}^{(i)},v_{y}^{(j)}\in V_{X}
with an edge in
E_{X}
if and only if

\neg(x=y\text{ and }i=j)\text{ and }d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)<R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right),

where the condition
\neg(x=y\text{ and }i=j)
prevents self-loops.

•

For each
i\in[q]
and each
x\in X
, we set
\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)=\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
.
Note that for all
x,y\in X
this means that
\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)=\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{y}^{(i)}}}\right)
, and we sometimes abuse notation and write
\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
instead.

We note that the multivariate hard-core representation
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
does not only depend on the set of points
X
but also on the hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
.
Since we fix the hard-constraint point process in advance, our notation is unambiguous in each context.

4.1
Properties of the hard-core model and independent multisets

We prove important properties of the multivariate hard-core model that we use in our main proofs.
Our first statement gives a trivial upper bound for the partition function.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
(G,\lambda)
be a multivariate hard-core instance.
It holds that

Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\sum_{v\in V}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}.

Proof.

The claim immediately follows by observing that

Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)\leq\sum_{S\subseteq V}\prod_{v\in S}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=\prod_{v\in V}(1+\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right))\leq\prod_{v\in V}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}.\qed

Next, we show that the multivariate hard-core partition function is log-subadditive in
\lambda
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
(G,\lambda_{1})
and
(G,\lambda_{2})
be two multivariate hard-core models on the same graph
G
.
Further, let us write
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}
for the function
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}\colon v\mapsto\lambda_{1}(v)+\lambda_{2}(v)
.
It holds that

Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda_{1}}}\right)\cdot Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda_{2}}}\right).

Proof.

First observe that

Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}}}\right)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\prod_{v\in I}(\lambda_{1}(v)+\lambda_{2}(v))=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\sum_{I_{1}\subseteq I}\prod_{v\in I_{1}}\lambda_{1}(v)\prod_{v\in I\setminus I_{1}}\lambda_{2}(v).

For every independent set
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
, it holds that all subsets of vertices
I_{1}\subseteq I
are also independent sets
I_{1}\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
.
Note that this also implies that
I\setminus I_{1}\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
.
Further, note that for every pair of independent sets
I_{1},I_{2}\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
, there is at most one
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
such that
I_{1}\cup I_{2}=I
.
Thus, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\sum_{I_{1}\subseteq I}\prod_{v\in I_{1}}\lambda_{1}(v)\prod_{v\in I\setminus I_{1}}\lambda_{2}(v)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{I_{1}\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\sum_{I_{2}\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\prod_{v\in I_{1}}\lambda_{1}(v)\prod_{v\in I_{2}}\lambda_{2}(v)

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda_{1}(v)}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda_{2}(v)}}\right)

\displaystyle=Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda_{1}}}\right)\cdot Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda_{2}}}\right),

which proves the claim.
∎

To bound the error between the partition function of a hard-constraint point process and its hard-core representation, we need to deal with the following problem:
The continuous process is capable to place particles very close to each other, as long as the respective particle types as not subjected to interactions according to the interaction matrix
H
.
On the other hand, in the hard-core representation, each vertex cannot be multiple times in the same independent set.
This means each combination of a point
x\in X
and a type
i\in[q]
can be represented at most once in every valid configuration.

In order to overcome the difference between both configuration spaces, we consider a modified version of the multivariate hard-core model, which assigns weights to independent multisets, that is, multisets of vertices that have an independent set as support.
Formally, we describe such a multiset by a tuple
(I,c)
where
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
and
c\colon I\to\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
.
Given a hard-core instance
(G,\lambda)
where for all
v\in V
, we have
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)<1
, we define the
multiset hard-core partition function
as

Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\sum_{c\colon I\to\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)^{c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}.

We note that we require for all
v\in V
that
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)<1
in order for the partition function to converge.
Further, note that there is always exactly one function
c\colon\emptyset\to\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
and that the empty product evaluates to
1
.
This ensures that the empty set contributes exactly
1
to the partition function.

We obtain the following upper and lower bound on
Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)
in terms of
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(G,\lambda)
be a multivariate hard-core instance, such that for all
v\in V
, it holds that
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
Further, let
\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}\colon v\mapsto\frac{\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}{1-\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}
and
2\lambda^{2}\colon v\mapsto 2\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)^{2}
.
Then

Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)=Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,2\lambda^{2}}}\right)\cdot Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{2\sum_{v\in V}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)^{2}}Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right).

Proof.

First, note that the first inequality is trivial, as the multiset partition function especially includes for each independent set
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)
the multiset
(I,c)
where
c
is constantly
1
.
The only exception to this is
I=\emptyset
, in which case the contribution to both partition functions is exactly
1
.

To derive the identity
Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)=Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}}}\right)
, we start by rewriting

Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\sum_{c\colon I\to\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)^{c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\sum_{c\colon I\to\mathds{N}}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)^{c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}.

As, for all
v\in V
, we have
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}<1
, we further get

\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\sum_{c\colon I\to\mathds{N}}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)^{c\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\prod_{v\in I}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\prod_{v\in I}\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)^{k}=\sum_{I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G}}\right)}\prod_{v\in I}\frac{\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}{1-\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)},

which is exactly the desired result.

Next, for all
v\in V
, observe that
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}
implies
\frac{\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}{1-\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+2\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)}}\right)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)=2\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)^{2}+\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)
.
Using
Lemma

4.2
, we obtain

Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,2\lambda^{2}}}\right)\cdot Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right).

Finally, applying
Lemma

4.1
to
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,2\lambda^{2}}}\right)
yields the last inequality.
∎

We note that while we primarily make use of the upper bound
\mathrm{e}^{2\sum_{v\in V}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)^{2}}Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)
, we believe that the intermediate bounds are of independent interest.
Note that the choice of
\frac{1}{2}
as an upper bound of
\lambda
in
Lemma

4.3
is rather arbitrary.
In fact, for any
a>0
, the upper bound
1-\frac{1}{a}
yields

Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,a\lambda^{2}}}\right)\cdot Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{a\sum_{v\in V}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v}}\right)^{2}}Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G,\lambda}}\right).

For our purpose, the bound of
\frac{1}{2}
is sufficient.

4.2
Bounding the discretization error

Our bound for the discretization error for any given finite non-empty point set
X\subseteq\mathds{V}
is closely related to the existence of a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation, defined as follows.

See
1.1

For
X\subset\mathds{V}
and a corresponding allocation
\Phi
, we relate the hard-core representation
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
to the continuous model
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
.
To this end, we consider two intermediate continuous models.

For
k\in\mathds{N}
and a tuple
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in[k]}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
we abuse notation and write
\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)
for the tuple
(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right))_{i\in[k]}\in X^{k}
.
The first intermediate partition function we consider is

Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

Intuitively, this is the partition function that results from mapping each point in
\mathds{V}
to the point in
X
that it is allocated to, before checking if a configuration is valid.

For the second intermediate model, let again
k\in\mathds{N}
.
For all
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in[k]}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
and
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
, let

\displaystyle D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]\colon\\
i<j\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))},v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{j}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right))}}}\right)\notin E_{X}\text{ and }v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}\neq v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{j}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right))}}}\right\}}\textrm{, and let}

\displaystyle w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x_{i}}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right).

Further, define the partition function and Gibbs density corresponding to
w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
, respectively, as

\displaystyle Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}\textrm{ and}

\displaystyle\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}{Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}.

In
Section

7
, we give a detailed probabilistic interpretation of
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
.
For this section and the approximation results in
Section

6
, we are mostly interested in
Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
.

The following bound is central to almost all results in this paper.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types, and assume
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
is star-convex.
Further, set
R_{\min}=\inf_{i,j\in[q]}\{R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\mid R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0\}
, and set
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
.
Let
X\subseteq\mathds{V}
with
4\lambda_{\max}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|<\infty
, and let
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
be the hard-core representation of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
based on
X
.
Last, let
\delta\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,\frac{1}{2}}}\right]
and
\varepsilon\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,\frac{R_{\min}}{2}}}\right]
, and assume that
\Phi
is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\delta+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}}}\right)\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

The proof of this statement is divided into two parts, which we state separately.
The first part essentially bounds the error that is introduced into the partition function of the point process if we map each point
x\in\mathds{V}
to
\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
, the point it is allocated to, before checking if a configuration is valid or not.
This essentially corresponds to relating the original point process to the first intermediate model.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types, and assume
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
is star-convex.
Further, set
R_{\min}=\inf_{i,j\in[q]}\{R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\mid R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0\}
, and set
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
.
Let
X\subseteq\mathds{V}
be finite, and let
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
be the hard-core representation of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
based on
X
.
Last, let
\delta\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,1}}\right]
and
\varepsilon\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,\frac{R_{\min}}{2}}}\right]
, and assume that
\Phi
is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
.
For all
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in[k]}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
write
\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)
for
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)_{i\in[k]}\in X^{k}
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

Proof.

The main idea of the proof is to show that

\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}\leq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R_{-},\lambda}}\right)-Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R_{+},\lambda}}\right),

where
R_{-}
and
R_{+}
are slightly scaled versions of
R
, and then applying
Lemma

3.5
.

First, note that for
k\leq 1
, it holds for all
\bm{x}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
and
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
that
w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)=w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)
.
Fix
k\geq 2
as well as
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
.
Let
N_{\tau}\subseteq\mathds{V}^{k}
be the set of all points
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in[k]}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
such that
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)\neq D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)
.
Observe that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\mathds{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\bm{x}\in N_{\tau}}}\right\}}.

We proceed by characterizing a superset of
N_{\tau}
that is easier to analyze.
Observe that, as
\Phi
is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation, it holds for all
x\in\mathds{V}
that
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)}}\right)\leq\varepsilon
.
Let
R_{-}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{2\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)R
and
R_{+}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{2\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)R
.
Thus, for all
i,j\in[q]
with
R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0
, it holds that
R_{-}(i,j)\leq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)-2\varepsilon
and
R_{+}(i,j)\geq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)+2\varepsilon
.

Let
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in[k]}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
such that
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{-}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=0
.
Note that this implies that there are
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
such that
R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}}\right)>0
and

d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i},x_{j}}}\right)<R_{-}(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right))\leq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}}\right)-2\varepsilon.

We conclude that
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i},x_{j}}}\right)<R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}}\right)
and by triangle inequality
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right),\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{j}}}\right)}}\right)<R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}}\right)
.
Thus, we have
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)=0
and consequently
\bm{x}\notin N_{\tau}
.

Next, let
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in[k]}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
with
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{+}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=1
.
Note that this implies for all
i,j\in[k]
with
i\neq j
and
R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}}\right)>0
that

d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i},x_{j}}}\right)\geq R_{+}(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right))\geq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}}\right)+2\varepsilon.

We conclude that
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i},x_{j}}}\right)\geq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}}\right)
and by triangle inequality
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right),\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{j}}}\right)}}\right)\geq R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right),\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}}\right)
.
Thus, we have
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)=1
and consequently
\bm{x}\notin N_{\tau}
.

Finally, observe that, because for all
i,j\in[q]
, it holds that
R_{-}(i,j)\leq R_{+}(i,j)
, we have that
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{-}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=0
implies
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{+}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=0
, and
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{+}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=1
implies
D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{-}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=1
.
Thus, we have

\mathds{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\bm{x}\in N_{\tau}}}\right\}}\leq D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{-}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)-D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{+}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right).

Substituting this into
equation

13
yields

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 2}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathds{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\bm{x}\in N_{\tau}}}\right\}}\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 2}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{-}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt-\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 2}}\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R_{+}}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R_{-},\lambda}}\right)-Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R_{+},\lambda}}\right).

Recalling the definitions of
R_{-}
and
R_{+}
as well as that
\mathds{V}
is star-convex, we apply
Lemma

3.5
with
\alpha=\frac{2\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}\in[0,1]
and obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right),

(6)

which concludes the proof.
∎

The second lemma relates the partition function that results from mapping each point
x\in\mathds{V}
to
\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
with
Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
.
This is, relating our first continuous intermediate model with the second one.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types.
Set
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
.
Let
X\subseteq\mathds{V}
with
4\lambda_{\max}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|<\infty
, and let
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
be the hard-core representation of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
based on
X
.
Last, let
\delta\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,\frac{1}{2}}}\right]
and
\varepsilon\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,1}}\right]
, and assume that
\Phi
is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
.
Then

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{2\delta\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

Proof.

We prove the statement by arguing that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},(1+\delta)\lambda_{X}}}\right)-Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},(1-\delta)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

and applying
Lemmas

4.1
,
4.2
and
4.3
.

For
\bm{x}=(x_{i})_{i\in[k]}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
and
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
, let

\widehat{D}_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i,j\in[k]\colon\\
i<j\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))},v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{j}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right))}}}\right)\notin E_{X}\text{ or }v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}=v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{j}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right))}}}\right\}}.

Note that for all
\bm{x}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
and all type assignments
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
, it holds that

\displaystyle D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\leq D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)}}\right)\leq\widehat{D}_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right).

Further, as
\Phi
is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
, it holds for all
x\in X
that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\leq\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}.

For all
x\in X
and
i\in[q]
, because
\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)=\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)}}\right)}\leq\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)}}\right)}.

This implies for all
\bm{x}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
and
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)\widehat{D}_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)

and

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)\widehat{D}_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right).

(7)

Substituting this into
equation

14
yields

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)\widehat{D}_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle-\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

(8)

Next, note that due to
\delta\leq 1
and
4\lambda_{\text{max}}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|
, it holds for all
v_{x}^{(i)}\in V_{X}
that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)\leq 2\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}.

(9)

Thus,
Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)
is well defined.
We proceed by rewriting both functions,
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)
and
Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)
, by expressing each in terms of tuples of vertices.
For
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)
, this yields

\displaystyle Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\sum_{\bm{x}\in X^{k}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x_{i}}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

(10)

Similarly, for
Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)
, we get

\displaystyle Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\sum_{\bm{x}\in X^{k}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x_{i}}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}}\right)\widehat{D}_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)\widehat{D}_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

(11)

Note that the multiset version always allows arbitrarily many copies of the same vertex, independent of
R
, which is captured by the indicator function
\widehat{D}_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}
.

Substituting
equations

10
and
11
into
equation

8
yields

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)-Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right).

We proceed by bounding both terms of the difference.
To this end, we start by upper-bounding the partition function
Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)
in terms of
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)
using
Lemma

4.3
(note that we can do so by
equation

9
).
We obtain

\displaystyle Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{2\sum_{i\in[q]}\sum_{x\in X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)^{2}\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)^{2}}Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)^{2}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\sum_{x\in X}\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|^{2}}}Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right).

Further, by
Lemmas

4.2
and
4.1
we obtain

\displaystyle Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},2\delta\lambda_{X}}}\right)Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{\sum_{i\in[q]}\sum_{x\in X}2\delta\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)}Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{2\delta\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle Z_{\text{MHC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},(1+\delta)\lambda_{X}}}\right)-Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},(1-\delta)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{2\delta\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right).

Next, by
equations

7
and
10
, we know that

\displaystyle Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle=Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{2\delta\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta}}\right)\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{2\delta\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right),

which concludes the proof.
∎

We now apply
Lemmas

4.5
and
4.6
to prove
Lemma

4.4
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.4
.

By
Lemma

4.5
we have

\displaystyle Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

Due to
Lemma

4.6
, this implies

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{2\delta\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

(12)

Further, by the triangle inequality, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

(13)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right),\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

(14)

Combining
Lemma

4.5
and
equation

12
we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\delta+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}}}\right)\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right),

which proves the claim.
∎

As
Lemma

4.4
immediately implies a bound on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)-Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}}\right|
, this leads us to the question how to compute
Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
efficiently.
To this end, the following identity will come in handy.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types and
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
.
Further, let
X\subseteq\mathds{V}
be finite and non-empty, and let
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
be the hard-core representation of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
based on
X
.
For all
\delta\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,1}}\right)
and
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, and all
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocations
\Phi
for
X
, we have

Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)=Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right).

Proof.

We prove this claim by rewriting
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
in terms of tuples of vertices, yielding

\displaystyle Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\sum_{\bm{x}\in X^{k}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x_{i}}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\frac{1}{k!}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\prod_{i\in[k]}\frac{\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}^{(\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right))}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x_{i}}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)}}}\right)D_{\tau}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\Phi}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x}}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle=Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right).\qed

Note that
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
is simply the partition function of the discrete hard-core model, which, as we discussed in the introduction, is well studied from a computational perspective.
Combining
Lemmas

4.4
and
4.7
immediately yields the following bound on the difference between
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
and
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
.

\DiscretisationError

We would like to add a remark about the error term, given in
Footnote

-0.1
.
While it seems to depend on three quantities, namely
\delta
,
\varepsilon
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|
, it usually suffices to focus on
\delta
and
\varepsilon
.
This is because decreasing
\varepsilon
requires to increase
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|
.
One way to see this is to observe that for any
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, a lower bound for the minimum size of
X\subset\mathds{V}
such that a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation exists is
\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\varepsilon}}\right)}}\right)}
.
Thus, choosing
\varepsilon
sufficiently small typically results in sufficiently large
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|
.

Another interesting aspect is the role of
\Phi
.
To construct the hard-core representation for the continuous model and apply
Footnote

-0.1
, it is sufficient to know a set of points
X\subset\mathds{V}
such that a suitable
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation exists.
However, the allocation itself is not required to be known for constructing the discretization.
This is because, even though
Lemma

4.4
seems to depend on the specific allocation
\Phi
,
Lemma

4.7
shows that
Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)=Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
independent of
\Phi
.
For our algorithmic results in
Section

6
, we focus on the case where
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
(i.e.,
\mathds{V}
is a hypercube).
In this case, we provide an explicit allocation.
Nevertheless, noting that an explicit construction is not required might be of its own interest when generalizing the results to different classes of continuous regions
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
.

5
Canonical discretization for cubic regions

As mentioned earlier, our algorithmic results focus on cubic regions
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
,
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Note that such regions are star-convex.
In what follows, we show how to construct, for any given
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, a set of points
X\subset\mathds{V}
such that there is a
0
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
and with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|
polynomial in
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
and
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
.
We refer to this as the
canonical point set
and to the resulting graph as the
canonical discretization
.

Formally, the canonical point set is parameterized by a resolution
\rho\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Let
\mathds{G}_{n}=[0,n)^{d}\cap\mathds{N}^{d}
be a
d
-dimensional positive integer grid.
For all cubic regions
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
,
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, we call
\rho\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
a
feasible resolution
if and only if
\ell\rho\in\mathds{N}
.
Given a feasible resolution
\rho\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, we define the canonical point set for resolution
\rho
as

X_{\rho}=\frac{1}{\rho}\mathds{G}_{\ell\rho}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(d)}}}\right)\in\mathds{V}\penalty\ \middle|\penalty\ \forall i\in[d]\exists m\in\mathds{N}\colon x^{(i)}=\frac{m}{\rho}}}\right\}.

For a hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
we now define the canonical discretization to be the hard-core representation
(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}})
based on
X_{\rho}
.
Note that in the special case of the hard-sphere model, this canonical discretization coincides to the procedure proposed in
[
FGK+21
]
.
However, our approach works for a much more general class of point processes and, as the rest of this section shows, we obtain much stronger error bounds on the partition function.

The following statement shows how the existence of an allocation for a canonical point set
X_{\rho}
depends on the resolution
\rho
.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, let
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
, and let
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
For all feasible resolutions
\rho\geq\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon}
there is a
0
-
\varepsilon
-allocation of
\mathds{V}
for the canonical point set
X_{\rho}
.

Proof.

We prove our claim by constructing an allocation
\Phi\colon\mathds{V}\to X_{\rho}
with the desired properties.
To this end, let
y=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(d)}}}\right)\in\mathds{V}
.
We set

\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\lfloor\rho y^{(1)}\rfloor}{\rho},\dots,\frac{\lfloor\rho y^{(d)}\rfloor}{\rho}}}\right).

That is,
y
gets mapped to the closest point in
x=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(d)}}}\right)\in X_{\rho}
such that
x^{(i)}\leq y^{(i)}
for all
i\in[d]
.

Now, let
x=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(d)}}}\right)\in X_{\rho}
and observe that

\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)=\prod_{i\in[d]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[x^{(i)},x^{(i)}+\frac{1}{\rho}}}\right),

where the product is meant to be the cartesian product.
Note that
\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
is measurable.
Further, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|=\rho^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
.
Thus, we have
