\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)}}\right)=\frac{1}{\rho^{d}}=\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\rho^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}=\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|}.

Finally, observe that for all
x\in X_{\rho}
and all
y\in\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
, it holds that

d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)\leq\sqrt{d\frac{1}{\rho^{2}}}=\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\rho}.

Thus, for
\rho\geq\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon}
we have
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)\leq\varepsilon
, which proves that
\Phi
is a
0
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X_{\rho}
and concludes the proof.
∎

We can now use the hard-core partition function of the canonical discretization as an approximation of the partition function of the hard-constraint point process as stated in the following result.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types and
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
For all
\varepsilon_{\text{D}}\in(0,1]
there exists
\rho_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}\in\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\varepsilon_{\text{D}}^{-1/d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{1/d}}}}\right)
such that for all feasible resolutions
\rho\geq\rho_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}
we have

\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

Proof.

Let
R_{\min}=\inf_{i,j\in[q]}\{R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\mid R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0\}
and
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
.
We set

\rho_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}=\sqrt{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{48q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\cdot\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\frac{4}{R_{\min}}}}\right\}.

First observe that
\rho_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}
satisfies the required asymptotic behavior.

Now, for all feasible resolutions
\rho\geq\rho_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}
we know by
Lemma

5.1
that there is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for the canonical point set
X_{\rho}
with
\delta=0
and

\varepsilon=\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\rho}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{48q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\cdot\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\frac{R_{\min}}{4}}}\right\}.

To prove our claim, we aim for applying
Footnote

-0.1
based on the canonical point set
X_{\rho}
and the
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation obtained from
Lemma

5.1
.
We start by checking that
\delta
,
\varepsilon
, and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|
satisfy the conditions of
Footnote

-0.1
.

To this end, note that it trivially holds that
\delta=0\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,\frac{1}{2}}}\right]
.
Further, observe that for sufficiently large
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
, it holds that
\varepsilon\in[0,R_{\min}/2]
.
Finally, note that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|=\rho^{d}\ell^{d}\geq d^{\frac{d}{2}}\frac{48q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}\cdot\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}}}\right\}.

Thus, for
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)\geq 1
we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|\geq 4\lambda_{\max}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right).

We can now apply
Footnote

-0.1
.
Recall that we have
\delta=0
.
Further, by

\varepsilon\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{48q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\cdot\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\frac{R_{\min}}{4}}}\right\}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{6\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\frac{R_{\min}}{4}

we get

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{6}.

Observe that for

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|\geq d^{\frac{d}{2}}\frac{48q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}\cdot\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}}}\right\}\geq\frac{48\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}

it holds that

\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{6}.

Thus, using
Footnote

-0.1
, we get

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)-Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}}\right|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{3}}-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

Note that
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{3}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}
and
2-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{3}}\geq\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}
for
\varepsilon_{\text{D}}\in(0,1]
.
Therefor, we have

\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right),

which concludes the proof.
∎

Note that the above statement works for
\delta=0
, meaning that we do not make full use of the power of
Footnote

-0.1
.
However, we use the theorem to its full extend when investigating discretizations based on (uniformly) random point sets in
Section

8
.
This does not directly lead to any new algorithmic results, but it points out an interesting relationship between the hard-core partition functions of random geometric graphs and continuous hard-constraint point processes, which we believe to be interesting in its own right.

For the rest of this section, we prove some properties of the canonical discretization that come in handy when deriving sufficient conditions for an efficient approximation of
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
via
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)
.
We start with the following observation that bounds the number of vertices in
G_{X_{\rho}}
.

Observation 5.3
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types and
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
For every feasible resolution
\rho\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
it holds that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|V_{X_{\rho}}}}\right|=q\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho\ell}}\right)^{d}=q\rho^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right).

Note that, by
5.3
,
Theorem

5.2
implies that we only need
\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}}}}\right)
vertices in order to ensure that
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)
is an
\varepsilon_{\text{D}}
-approximation of
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
.
Considering for instance the hard-sphere model, this improves the required number of vertices from super-exponential in
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
to quadratic, compared to
[
FGK+21
]
.

Another important property of the canonical discretization are the vertex degrees.
Especially upper bounding those degrees is closely related to our ability to approximate the hard-core partition function.
To analyze how the degrees increase in terms of the resolution
\rho
, we apply the following lemma.

Lemma 5.4
.

For
d\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
and
s\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, let
b_{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s}}\right)
denote the number of integer points in the ball
\mathds{B}_{\mathbf{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s}}\right)
, where
\mathbf{0}
is the origin of
\mathds{R}^{d}
.
Further, let
\gamma\in(0,1]
.
For all
s\geq\frac{2d^{3/2}}{\gamma}
it holds that

b_{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s}}\right)\leq(1+\gamma)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s}}\right)}}\right).

Proof.

Consider
\mathds{B}_{\mathbf{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s+\sqrt{d}}}\right)
and observe that for every integer point
(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(d)})\in\mathds{B}_{\mathbf{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s}}\right)\cap\mathds{Z}^{d}
it holds that the hypercube
[x^{(1)},x^{(1)}+1)\times\dots\times[x^{(d)},x^{(d)}+1)
of volume
1
is completely contained in
\mathds{B}_{\mathbf{0}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s+\sqrt{d}}}\right)
.
Further, note that for different integer points, the corresponding hypercubes are disjoint.
Thus, we obtain

b_{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s}}\right)\leq\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s+\sqrt{d}}}\right)}}\right).

It remains to bound the volume
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s+\sqrt{d}}}\right)}}\right)
to get the desired result.
First, note that

\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s+\sqrt{d}}}\right)}}\right)=\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s}}\right)}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\sqrt{d}}{s}}}\right)^{d}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\frac{d^{3/2}}{s}}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(s}}\right)}}\right).

By
s\geq\frac{2d^{3/2}}{\gamma}
we have
\frac{d^{3/2}}{s}\leq 1
and
\frac{2d^{3/2}}{s}\leq\gamma
.
Thus, we get

\mathrm{e}^{\frac{d^{3/2}}{s}}\leq 1+\frac{2d^{3/2}}{s}\leq 1+\gamma

which concludes the proof.
∎

Before we use this lemma to bound the vertex degrees in
G_{X_{\rho}}
, we need to introduce some additional notation.
Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types and
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
For any feasible resolution
\rho\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
and any vertex
v_{x}^{(i)}\in V_{X_{\rho}}
we write
\Delta_{X_{\rho}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)
for the number of neighbors of
v_{x}^{(i)}
in
G_{X_{\rho}}
.
Further, for any
j\in[q]
, we write
\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)
to denote the number of neighbors of
v_{x}^{(i)}
that are from
V_{X_{\rho}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}
.
The following lemma gives a bound on the vertex degrees in the canonical discretization in terms of the resolution
\rho
and the volume exclusion matrix
B
of the model.

Lemma 5.5
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types and
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Let
B
be the corresponding volume exclusion matrix.
Set
R_{\min}=\inf_{i,j\in[q]}\{R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\mid R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0\}
and let
\gamma\in(0,1]
.
For all feasible resolutions
\rho\geq\frac{2d^{3/2}}{\gamma R_{\min}}
we have for all
i,j\in[q]
and
x\in X_{\rho}
that

\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)\leq(1+\gamma)\rho^{d}B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)

and consequently

\Delta_{X_{\rho}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)\leq(1+\gamma)\rho^{d}\sum_{j\in[q]}B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right).

Proof.

First, note that the bound on
\Delta_{X_{\rho}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)
follows immediately from the bound on
\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)
as the vertex sets
V_{X_{\rho}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}
for
j\in[q]
are a partitioning of
V_{X_{\rho}}
.

We proceed by bounding
\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)
.
Let
y\in X_{\rho}
and observe that there is only an edge between
v_{x}^{(i)}
and
v_{y}^{(j)}
in
G_{X_{\rho}}
if
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)<R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)
.
Further, note that for every fixed
i,j\in[q]
and every
x\in X_{\rho}
there are at most
b_{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)}}\right)
points
y\in X_{\rho}
such that
d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)<R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)
.
This is

\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)\leq b_{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)}}\right).

Further, note that for
\rho\geq\frac{2d^{3/2}}{\gamma R_{\min}}
we have
\rho R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\geq\frac{2d^{3/2}}{\gamma}
.
Thus, using
Lemma

5.4
, we get

\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)\leq(1+\gamma)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\rho R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)}}\right)}}\right)=(1+\gamma)\rho^{d}B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right),

which proves the claim.
∎

5.1
Tightness of the error bound

In what follows, we argue that the bound for the number of points, required for the canonical discretization, might be considered as asymptotically tight in the volume
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
and the discretization error
\varepsilon_{\text{D}}
.
Recall that, by
Theorem

5.2
, we require a resolution of
\rho\in\upTheta\big({\varepsilon_{\text{D}}^{-1/d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{-1/d}}\big)
, resulting in
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|\in\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\varepsilon_{\text{D}}^{-1}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}}}\right)
points to ensure that

\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

To argue that this is in general optimal, we construct a simple case in which we actually require
\upOmega\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\varepsilon_{\text{D}}^{-1}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}}}\right)
.
Consider a hard-constrain point process
(\mathds{V},R_{0},\lambda)
with
q=1
particle type and
R_{0}
being the
1\times 1
-matrix with entry
0
.
That is,
(\mathds{V},R_{0},\lambda)
is a simple Poisson point process on
\mathds{V}
with intensity
\lambda
(we treat
\lambda
as a constant here and omit the function notation).
The partition function of
(\mathds{V},R_{0},\lambda)
is then

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R_{0},\lambda}}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}.

Note that for all
n\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
and all
X\subset\mathds{V}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|=n
, it holds that

Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\lambda_{X}}}\right)^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\lambda\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{n}}}\right)^{n},

independent of the specific set of points
X
.

We proceed by lower-bounding the multiplicative error between these two partition functions.
The following elementary bound helps us to do so.

Lemma 5.6
.

For all
x,y\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
with
y\geq x
, it holds that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x}{y}}}\right)^{y}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x^{2}}{6y}}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{x}.

Proof.

First note that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x}{y}}}\right)^{y}=\mathrm{e}^{y\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x}{y}}}\right)}.

Further, as
0\leq\frac{x}{y}\leq 1
, we use the Taylor expansion at
1
and obtain

\displaystyle\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x}{y}}}\right)=\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}}(-1)^{k+1}\frac{x^{k}}{ky^{k}}\leq\frac{x}{y}-\frac{x^{2}}{2y^{2}}+\frac{x^{3}}{3y^{3}}\leq\frac{x}{y}-\frac{x^{2}}{6y^{2}}.

Thus, we conclude that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x}{y}}}\right)^{y}\leq\mathrm{e}^{y\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{x}{y}-\frac{x^{2}}{6y^{2}}}}\right)}=\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x^{2}}{6y}}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{x}.\qed

The following statement is an implication of
Lemma

5.6
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
x>6
and
\varepsilon_{\text{D}}\in(0,1]
.
For all
y\in\big(0,\frac{x^{2}}{6\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}\big)
, it holds that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x}{y}}}\right)^{y}<\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{x}.

Proof.

Let
y_{0}\in[x,\frac{x^{2}}{2\varepsilon_{\text{D}}})
and observe that by
Lemma

5.6
we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x}{y_{0}}}}\right)^{y_{0}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{x^{2}}{6y_{0}}}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{x}<\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{x}.

Further, let
y\in(0,x)
and note that
y<y_{0}
.
Thus, by Bernoullis inequality, we have

1+\frac{x}{y}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x}{y_{0}}}}\right)^{\frac{y_{0}}{y}}.

It follows that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x}{y}}}\right)^{y}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{x}{y_{0}}}}\right)^{y_{0}}<\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{x},

which proves the claim.
∎

We obtain the following result.

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R_{0},\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q=1
particle type and
R_{0}
being the
1\times 1
-matrix with entry
0
.
Assume that
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)>\frac{6}{\lambda}
.
For all
\varepsilon_{\text{D}}\in(0,1]
and all non-empty
X\subset\mathds{V}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|=n<\frac{\lambda^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}{6\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}
, it holds that

Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)<\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R_{0},\lambda}}\right),

meaning that
Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
is
not
an
\varepsilon_{\text{D}}
-approximation of
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R_{0},\lambda}}\right)
.

Proof.

Recall that

Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R_{0},\lambda}}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}\text{ and }Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\lambda\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{n}}}\right)^{n}.

The claim follows from applying
Lemma

5.7
with
x=\lambda\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)>6
and
y=n\in\big(0,\frac{x^{2}}{6\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}\big)
.
∎

We would like to add a few remarks about
Proposition

5.8
.
Firstly, note that it applies to all measurable and bounded regions
\mathds{V}
with sufficiently large volume.
Thus, it especially holds for cubic regions
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
as investigated in
Theorem

5.2
.
Furthermore, the considered hard-constraint point process is the most simple one that can be constructed, namely a point process without any constraints.
This means,
Proposition

5.8
does not give a lower bound for the number of points, required for discretizing any given hard-constraint point process.
However, it gives a lower bound for the general class of models, and intuition suggests that additional constraints should not decrease the number of points that are required.
Finally, the result only applies to our method of discretization.
That is, it proves that our bounds for the number of points are tight for our discretization method, but it does not exclude the existence of more efficient discretizations.

6
Efficient approximation

In this section, we investigate sufficient conditions for approximating the partition function of hard-constraint point processes on cubic regions
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
with
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, using the canonical discretization proposed in
Section

5
.
The characterization of cases for which we give an efficient approximation of the partition function is based on the volume exclusion matrix

B

, defined in
Section

3
.
We split this up into two main parts.

First, we investigate a condition that is especially useful in the setting of uniform fugacities (i.e.,
\lambda
is a constant function on
[q]
).
In this setting, the canonical discretization results in a univariate hard-core model, which allows us to apply the variety of algorithmic tools that are known for this model.

In the second part, we focus on the case of non-uniform fugacities.
Our algorithms in this setting are based on tools that where initially developed for abstract polymer models, which are multivariate hard-core models.

6.1
Uniform fugacity

Consider a hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types where the fugacity
\lambda
is a constant function on
[q]
.
To simplify notation, we use
\lambda
to denote the value of that function and omit the function notation here.

Note that for any fixed finite non-empty set of points
X\subseteq\mathds{V}
with corresponding hard-core representation
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
this implies that
\lambda_{X}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)=\lambda\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}
for all
x\in X
and
i\in[q]
.
That is,
\lambda_{X}
is a constant function on
V_{X}
and
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
is a univariate hard-core model.
For the sake of brevity, we write
\lambda_{X}=\lambda\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}
to denote that constant.

Based on
Theorem

2.2
and
Theorem

2.3
we can now obtain our main approximation result for the partition function of hard-constraint point processes with uniform fugacities.

\uniformapprox

Proof.

Let

\lambda=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\alpha}}\right)\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}}

for some
\alpha\in(0,1]
.
Set
\gamma=\alpha^{\prime}=\frac{\alpha}{2}
.

By
Theorem

5.2
we know that we can choose a resolution
\rho\in\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\varepsilon_{\text{A}}^{-1/d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{1/d}}}}\right)
such that the canonical discretization
(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}})
satisfies

\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}{2}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}{2}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

To approximate the hard-core partition function of
(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}})
we aim for applying
Theorem

2.2
and
Theorem

2.3
.
Thus, we need to bound the maximum degree of
G_{X_{\rho}}
and compare
\lambda_{X_{\rho}}
with the corresponding tree threshold.

Set
R_{\min}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}\{R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\mid R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0\}
and note that, for
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
sufficiently large, we can assume that
\rho\geq\frac{2d^{3/2}}{\gamma R_{\min}}
.
Thus, by
Lemma

5.5
we have

\Delta_{X_{\rho}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)\leq(1+\gamma)\rho^{d}\sum_{j\in[q]}B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right).

Let
\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{*}
denote the maximum degree in
G_{X_{\rho}}
and observe that

\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{*}=\max_{x\in X_{\rho},i\in[q]}\Delta_{X_{\rho}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)\leq(1+\gamma)\rho^{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}.

Further, note that

\lambda_{X_{\rho}}=\lambda\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|}=\frac{\lambda}{\rho^{d}}

and so we get

\lambda_{X_{\rho}}=(1-\alpha)\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\rho^{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}}\leq(1-\alpha^{\prime})\frac{\mathrm{e}}{(1+\gamma)\rho^{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}}\leq(1-\alpha^{\prime})\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{*}}.

Now, observe that
\lambda_{\text{c}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta}}\right)
converges to
\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\Delta}
from above for large
\Delta
.
Thus, we obtain

\lambda_{X_{\rho}}\leq(1-\alpha^{\prime})\lambda_{\text{c}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{*}}}\right),

where
\alpha^{\prime}>0
.

Using
5.3
, we know that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|V_{X_{\rho}}}}\right|=q\rho^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)\in\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}}}\right).

By
Theorem

2.2
we can immediately conclude the existence of a randomized approximation within the desired running time bounds.
Further, note that

\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{*}\leq(1+\gamma)\rho^{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}\in\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}}}}\right).

Thus, applying
Theorem

2.3
we obtain the deterministic approximation, which concludes the proof.
∎

We now demonstrate which bounds can be obtained based on
Section

1.3
for models commonly studied in statistical physics.
We start with the monoatomic hard-sphere model.

\monoatomichs

Section

1.3
follows from
Section

1.3
by observing that the interaction matrix corresponding to the hard-sphere model is a
1\times 1
matrix containing the value
2r
and that
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}=\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2r}}\right)}}\right)=2^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)
.
Due to the fact that we improved the number of points, required for discretization, from super-exponential in
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
to quadratic, we can run Glauber dynamics in order to obtain the approximation.
This greatly simplifies the discretization based randomized algorithm, introduced by
[
FGK+21
]
.
Further, we present the first efficient deterministic approximation.
This partially answers an open question, posed by
[
MP21a
]
.

\uniformwr

Section

1.3
follows from
Section

1.3
by observing that the interaction matrix is a
q\times q
matrix, which is
0
on the diagonal and
2r
otherwise.
To the best of our knowledge, no rigorous computational result for this model has been known before.

Remark 6.1
.

In fact, based on
Remark

2.4
we can apply
Section

1.3
also in the non-uniform setting when the condition is replaced by

\lambda_{\max}<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}},

where
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
.

6.2
Non-uniform fugacity

We proceed by looking at which conditions for efficient approximation we can get for hard-constraint point processes with non-uniform fugacities based on
Theorem

2.6
.

\nonUniformApprox

Proof.

We prove the theorem by applying
Theorem

2.6
to the canonical discretization
(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}})
with a sufficiently large resolution
\rho
.
To this end, we construct a function
g\colon V_{X_{\rho}}\to\mathds{R}_{>0}
that satisfies the condition of
Theorem

2.6
.

Assume that for all
i\in[q]
we have

f(i)\geq\alpha_{i}\sum_{j\in[q]}B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)f(j)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)

for some
\alpha_{i}>1
and set
\alpha=\min_{i\in[q]}\alpha_{i}
.
By
Theorem

5.2
, we know that we can choose a resolution in
\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\varepsilon_{\text{A}}^{-1/d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{1/d}}}}\right)
such that

\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}{2}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{A}}}{2}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right).

Set
R_{\min}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}\{R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\mid R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0\}
and note that, for
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
sufficiently large, it holds that
\rho\geq\frac{2d^{3/2}}{(\alpha-1)R_{\min}}
.
Thus, by
Lemma

5.5
, we know that for all
x\in X_{\rho}
and
i,j\in[q]
it holds that

\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)\leq\alpha\rho^{d}B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right).

Define a function
g\colon V_{X_{\rho}}\to\mathds{R}_{>0}
with
g\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)=f(i)
for all
i\in[q]
and all
x\in X_{\rho}
.
For all
i\in[q]
and all
x\in X_{\rho}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{w\in N\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)}g(w)\frac{\lambda_{X_{\rho}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)}{1+\lambda_{X_{\rho}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(w}}\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in[q]}\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right)f(j)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)\rho^{-d}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\sum_{j\in[q]}B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)f(j)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq f(i)

\displaystyle\leq g\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(v_{x}^{(i)}}}\right).

Thus, we can apply
Theorem

2.6
to obtain the desired approximation.
∎

In the case of uniform fugacities, the approximation result obtained from
Section

1.3
is worse than the one obtained from
Section

1.3
.
However, in the non-uniform case,
Section

1.3
can lead to interesting parameter regimes that are not covered by
Section

1.3
.
To demonstrate that, we look into a Widom–Rowlinson model with two particle types of identical radii but different fugacities.

\unbalancedWR

Proof.

Assume

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\alpha}}\right)\frac{1}{4^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)^{2}}

(15)

for some
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
.
We construct the equivalent hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
with
q=2
particle types, where
H
is a
2\times 2
matrix with
0
on the diagonal and
1
for all off-diagonal entries and
\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)=\lambda_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,2\}
.
Note that
equation

15
,
2^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)=\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2r}}\right)}}\right)
, and the definition of

B

imply that

B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,1}}\right)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1,2}}\right)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2}}\right)\leq 1-\alpha.

Set
f(1)=1
and
f(2)=(1+\beta)B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,1}}\right)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)
for some
\beta\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\frac{\alpha}{1-\alpha}}}\right)
.
Observe that

B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,1}}\right)f(1)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)=B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,1}}\right)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)<f(2)

and

B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1,2}}\right)f(2)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2}}\right)=(1+\beta)B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2,1}}\right)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1}}\right)B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1,2}}\right)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2}}\right)\leq(1+\beta)(1-\alpha)<f(1).

Applying
Section

1.3
concludes the proof.
∎

In the balanced case
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\lambda
,
Section

1.3
gives a bound on
\lambda
that is worse by a factor of
\mathrm{e}
compared to what we can get from
Section

1.3
and
Remark

6.1
(compare
Section

1.3
).
However, by using
Section

1.3
we see that we can make one of the two fugacities much larger, as long as we scale down the other one appropriately.
For example, according to
Section

1.3
we can get a polynomial time randomized approximation for
\lambda_{1}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{2}}{2^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)}
and
\lambda_{2}<\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}^{2}2^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)}
, which is obviously not possible using
Section

1.3
and
Section

1.3
.
This leads to an efficient approximation as long as the fugacities are sufficiently unbalanced.

7
Sampling via random perturbations

So far, we only presented approximation results for the partition function
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
of a hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
.
As we briefly discussed in the introduction, when it comes to sampling from the Gibbs distribution
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}
, a natural barrier is that outputting a sample (i.e., a tuple of points
\bm{x}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
and a type assignment
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
that represents a valid configuration) would, in fact, require infinite floating-point precision.
If we allow ourselves to use a computational model that can handle floating-point values with arbitrary precision, we can actually use our hard-core representation
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
to recover an approximate sampler for
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}
, given that we know a suitable allocation
\Phi
for
X
. This is captured by the following assumption, required for the algorithmic results of this section.

Assumption 7.1
.

The computational model can, in constant time,

•

output floating-point numbers with arbitrary precision,

•

perform basic arithmetic operations (addition, subtraction, …) on floating-point numbers with arbitrary precision,

•

compare floating-point numbers with arbitrary precision, and

•

produce, for all
a,b\in\mathds{R}
with
a<b
, a uniformly random floating-point number from the intervals
[a,b]
,
[a,b)
,
(a,b]
, and
(a,b)
.

This assumption is only required to give meaningful theoretical error bounds in terms of total-variation distance, which are impossible to achieve otherwise.

We proceed by describing our sampling procedure for a given hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
.
Let
X\subset\mathds{V}
be finite and non-empty and let
\Phi
be an allocation for
X
.
From
Section

4
, recall the definition of the intermediate continuous model, defined via the weight function
w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
as well as the corresponding Gibbs density
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
and partition function
Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)
.
Instead of directly sampling from
Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
, we approximately sample from
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
.
To this end, we first bound the total-variation distance between these two distributions.
The following lemma helps us use
Lemma

4.4
for this purpose.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types.
Further, let
X\subset\mathds{V}
be finite and non-empty and, for
\delta\in[0,1)
and
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, let
\Phi
be a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
.
It holds that

d_{\text{TV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)},\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}}}\right)\leq\frac{1}{Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

Proof.

First, note that the total-variation distance can be written as

\displaystyle d_{\text{TV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)},\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}}}\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.

(16)

For all
\bm{x}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
and
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
, observe that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}{Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}-\frac{w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}{Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}}}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt=\frac{1}{Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-\frac{Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}{Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 30.00005pt\leq\frac{1}{Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\frac{Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}{Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}-1}}\right|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right),

where the last inequality follows from the triangle inequality.
Substituting into
equation

16
, we have

\displaystyle d_{\text{TV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)},\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\Bigg(\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

\displaystyle\hskip 73.97733pt+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)-Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}}\right|\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k},

where the last inequality comes from the fact that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)-Z_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}}\right|\leq\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}.\qed

Note that under the conditions of
Lemma

4.4
,
Lemma

7.2
immediately gives a bound for the total-variation distance between
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}
and
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
.

Input:

hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types

Input:
finite and non-empty
X\subset\mathds{V}

Input:
hard-core representation
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
based on
X

Input:
independent set
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X}}}\right)
of cardinality
k

Input:
allocation
\Phi\colon\mathds{V}\to X

Output:

\bm{x}\in\mathds{V}^{k}

Output:
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]

construct a permutation
\big(v_{y_{i}}^{(\tau_{i})}\big)_{i\in[k]}
of the vertices in
I
uniformly at random;

1

for

i\in[k]

do

x_{i}\leftarrow
a point from
\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y_{i}}}\right)
, drawn uniformly at random;

2

\bm{x}\leftarrow(x_{i})_{i\in[k]}
;

3

for

i\in[k]

do

\tau\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\leftarrow\tau_{i}
;

4

Algorithm 1

The perturbation algorithm.
It takes an independent set of a given hard-core representation of a hard-constraint point process and an allocation, and it returns a perturbation of the given independent set with respect to the allocation.

In order to use
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
as an approximation for
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}
, we use
Algorithm

1
.
This algorithm adds random perturbations to a sample from the discretized distribution
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}
, based on
\Phi^{-1}
.
Applying
Algorithm

1
shows a connection between
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}
and
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
.

Observation 7.3
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types.
Further, let
X\subset\mathds{V}
be finite and non-empty and, for
\delta\in[0,1)
and
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, let
\Phi
be a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
.
Let
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
be the hard-core representation of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
based on
X
, and let
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X}}}\right)
be drawn according to
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}
.
Consider a tuple of points
\bm{x}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
and a type assignment
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
produced by applying
Algorithm

1
to
I
.
Then
(\bm{x},\tau)
is distributed according to the density
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
.

The observation follows directly from writing down the resulting density of
(\bm{x},\tau)
.

In practice, we might not have the ability to exactly sample an independent set of
G_{X}
according to
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}
.
The following lemma extends
7.3
to the setting where we have access to an approximate sampler for
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}
.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types.
Further, let
X\subset\mathds{V}
be finite and non-empty and, for
\delta\in[0,1)
and
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
, let
\Phi
be a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
.
Let
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
be the hard-core representation of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
based on
X
.
For
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}\in[0,1]
, let
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X}}}\right)
be drawn according to an
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
-approximation of
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}
.
Consider a tuple of points
\bm{x}\in\mathds{V}^{k}
and a type assignment
\tau\colon[k]\to[q]
produced by applying
Algorithm

1
to
I
.
Then
(\bm{x},\tau)
is distributed according to an
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
-approximation of
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
.

Proof.

Let
\widehat{\mu}_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}
be the given
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
-approximation for
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}
, and let
\widehat{\mu}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
be the resulting distribution over configuration
(\bm{x},\tau)
.
We prove our claim by constructing a coupling of
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
and
\widehat{\mu}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
such that they produce the same
(\bm{x},\tau)
with probability at least
1-\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
.
This results in the desired bound on the total-variation distance by the coupling lemma (see
[
Den12
, Theorem
2.4
]
).

To construct this coupling of
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
and
\widehat{\mu}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
, we start by considering an optimal coupling between
\widehat{\mu}_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}
and
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}
(see
[
Den12
, Theorem
2.12
]
).
For a tuple
(I,I^{\prime})\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X}}}\right)\times\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X}}}\right)
drawn from that optimal coupling, it holds that
I=I^{\prime}
with probability
1-\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
.
If this is the case, we produce
(\bm{x},\tau)
by applying
Algorithm

1
to
I=I^{\prime}
, and we return the tuple of configurations
((\bm{x},\tau),(\bm{x},\tau))
.
However, if we draw
I\neq I^{\prime}
, then we produce corresponding configurations
(\bm{x},\tau)
by applying
Algorithm

1
to
I
, and
(\bm{x^{\prime}},\tau^{\prime})
by applying
Algorithm

1
to
I^{\prime}
, and return
((\bm{x},\tau),(\bm{x^{\prime}},\tau^{\prime}))
.

Note that the description above gives a valid coupling of
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
and
\widehat{\mu}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
, as it preserves the marginal distributions.
Further, note that for a tuple
((\bm{x},\tau),(\bm{x^{\prime}},\tau^{\prime}))
produced by this coupling, it holds that
(\bm{x},\tau)=(\bm{x^{\prime}},\tau^{\prime})
if
I=I^{\prime}
in the first step.
This happens with probability at least
1-\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
, which proves the desired bound on the total-variation distance.
∎

To state some explicit approximate sampling results while keeping our statements more simple, we restrict ourselves once again to cubic regions
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
First, we derive a bound on the resolution
\rho
that we need for the canonical discretization to obtain a desired total-variation distance between
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}
and
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types and
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
For all
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}\in(0,1]
, there exists
\rho_{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}\in\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\varepsilon_{\text{S}}^{-1/d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{1/d}}}}\right)
such that for all feasible resolutions
\rho\geq\rho_{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}
, there is an allocation
\Phi
for
X_{\rho}
such that

d_{\text{TV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)},\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)}_{\Phi}}}\right)\leq\varepsilon_{\text{S}}.

Proof.

Most of the proof is similar to the proof of
Theorem

5.2
.
Let
R_{\min}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}\{R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\mid R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0\}
, let
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
, set

\rho_{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}=\sqrt{d}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{32q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\cdot\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\frac{4}{R_{\min}}}}\right\},

and observe that
\rho_{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}
satisfies the asymptotic assumptions of the statement.

For all feasible resolutions
\rho\geq\rho_{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}
, let
\Phi
be an allocation for the canonical point set
X_{\rho}
as constructed in the proof of
Lemma

5.1
.
This especially means that
\Phi
is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X_{\rho}
with
\delta=0
and

\varepsilon=\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\rho}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{32q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\cdot\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\frac{R_{\min}}{4}}}\right\}.

We prove our claim, by combining
Lemmas

4.4
and
7.2
based on the canonical point set
X_{\rho}
and the
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation
\Phi
.
First, we check that
\delta
,
\varepsilon
, and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|
satisfy the conditions of
Footnote

-0.1
.

It trivially holds that
\delta=0\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,\frac{1}{2}}}\right]
.
Moreover, we have
\varepsilon\in[0,R_{\min}/2]
for
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
sufficiently large.
Observe that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|=\rho^{d}\ell^{d}\geq d^{\frac{d}{2}}\frac{32q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}\cdot\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}}}\right\}.

Thus, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|\geq 4\lambda_{\max}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
.

We proceed by upper-bounding

\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}

via
Footnote

-0.1
.
Recall that we have
\delta=0
.
Further, note that

\varepsilon\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{32q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\cdot\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\frac{R_{\min}}{4}}}\right\}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{4\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\frac{R_{\min}}{4}

and thus

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{4}.

Moreover, for

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|\geq d^{\frac{d}{2}}\frac{32q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}\cdot\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}}}\right\}\geq\frac{32\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}},

it holds that

\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{\rho}}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{4}.

By
Footnote

-0.1
, we obtain

\sum_{k\in\mathds{N}}\sum_{\tau\colon[k]\to[q]}\int_{\mathds{V}^{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(R,\lambda}}\right)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)-w^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\bm{x},\tau}}\right)}}\right|\,\text{d}\nu^{d\times k}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}}-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right),

and, by
Lemma

7.2
, this implies
d_{\text{TV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)},\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)}_{\Phi}}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}}-1
.

Finally, observe that, for
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}\in(0,1]
, it holds that
\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}}-1\leq\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We now state the sampling analogue of
Section

1.3
and
Section

1.3
.
We start with the case of uniform fugacities.

\uniformSampling

Proof.

The rough proof sketch is to choose the resolution
\rho
and the canonical point set
X_{\rho}
with allocation
\Phi
that such
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
is an
\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}
-approximation of
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}
.
We then sample
\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}
-approximately from
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)}
to obtain an
\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}
-approximation of
\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)}_{\Phi}
.
Obviously, the resulting error is then bounded by
\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}=\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
.

By
Lemma

7.5
, there is a resolution
\rho\in\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\varepsilon_{\text{S}}^{-1/d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{1/d}}}}\right)
such that

d_{\text{TV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)},\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)}_{\Phi}}}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2},

where, as described in
Lemma

5.1
,

\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)=\prod_{i\in[d]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[x^{(i)},x^{(i)}+\frac{1}{\rho}}}\right).

Note that the corresponding graph
G_{X_{X}}
has
\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}}}}\right)
vertices.

Further, analogously to the proof of
Section

1.3
, if
\lambda<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}}
, constructing the hard-core representation
(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}})
can be done in such a way that
\lambda_{X_{\rho}}<\lambda_{\text{c}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Delta_{X_{\rho}}^{*}}}\right)
.
Thus, by
Theorem

2.1
, we can sample
\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}
-approximately from
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)}
in time
\mathrm{poly}\Big({\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|V_{X_{\rho}}}}\right|}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}}\Big)
, which is also in
\mathrm{poly}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}}}}\right)
.

We now obtain our sampler by drawing an independent set
I\in\mathcal{I}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)
according to this
\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}
-approximation of
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)}
and applying
Algorithm

1
to it.
To this end, note that for all
y\in X_{\rho}
, a uniform sample
x\in\Phi^{-1}{y}
is obtained by generating, for each
i\in[d]
, a uniformly random floating-point number
z^{(i)}\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[0,\frac{1}{\rho}}}\right)
and setting
x=y+(z^{(i)})_{i\in[d]}
, where the addition is component-wise.

Lemma

7.4
yields the desired bound on the total-variation distance.
∎

The sampling analogues of
Sections

1.3
and
1.3
follow immediately.

Corollary 7.6
.

Suppose a computational model that satisfies
7.1
.
Let
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Further, let
r\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
and
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Denote by
\mu_{\text{HS}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda}}\right)}
the Gibbs distribution of the hard-sphere model on
\mathds{V}
with particles of radius
r
and fugacity
\lambda
.
If

\lambda<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{2^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)},

then, for all
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}\in(0,1]
, there is an
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
-approximate sampler for
\mu_{\text{HS}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda}}\right)}
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\big({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}}\big)
.

Corollary 7.7
.

Suppose a computational model that satisfies
7.1
.
Let
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Further, let
r\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
,
\lambda\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
and
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
.
Denote by
\mu_{\text{WR}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda}}\right)}
the Gibbs distribution of the Widom–Rowlinson model on
\mathds{V}
with
q
particle types, each of radius
r
and fugacity
\lambda
.
If

\lambda<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{(q-1)2^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)},

then, for all
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}\in(0,1]
, there is an
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
-approximate sampler for
\mu_{\text{WR}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda}}\right)}
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\big({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}}\big)
.

Remark 7.8
.

Analogously to
Section

1.3
, letting
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
,
Section

1.4
extends to the non-uniform setting when the condition is replaced by

\lambda_{\max}<\frac{\mathrm{e}}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lVert B}}\right\rVert_{1}}.

Just as for
Section

1.3
, we also state a sampling version of
Section

1.3
under
7.1
.

\nonUniformSampling

Proof.

As in the proof of
Section

1.4
, we start by using
Lemma

7.5
, by which there exists a resolution
\rho\in\upTheta\big({\varepsilon_{\text{S}}^{-1/d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{1/d}}\big)
such that

d_{\text{TV}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)},\mu^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)}_{\Phi}}}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2},

where again

\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)=\prod_{i\in[d]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[x^{(i)},x^{(i)}+\frac{1}{\rho}}}\right).

The corresponding graph
G_{X_{X}}
consists of
\upTheta\big({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}}\big)
vertices.

Analogously to the proof of
Section

1.3
, there is a function
f\colon[q]\to\mathds{R}_{>0}
such that for all
i\in[q]
, it holds that
f(i)>\sum_{j\in[q]}B\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)f(j)\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(j}}\right)
, which implies that there is also a function
g\colon V_{X_{\rho}}\to\mathds{R}_{>0}
that satisfies the condition of
Theorem

2.5
.
Thus, we can sample
\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}
-approximately from
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)}
in the desired running time.
Similarly to the proof of
Section

1.4
, applying
Algorithm

1
to independent sets drawn from this
\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}{2}
-approximation of
\mu_{\text{HC}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X_{\rho}},\lambda_{X_{\rho}}}}\right)}
yields the desired sampler, and by
Lemma

7.4
we obtain the required bound on the total-variation distance.
∎

The sampling version of
Section

1.3
follows.

Corollary 7.9
.

Suppose a computational model that satisfies
7.1
.
Let
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
Further, let
r\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
,
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathds{R}_{\geq 0}
.
Denote by
\mu_{\text{WR}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}}\right)}
the Gibbs distribution of the Widom–Rowlinson model on
\mathds{V}
with
2
particle types, both with the same radius
r
but (possibly) different fugacities
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
.
If

\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}<\frac{1}{4^{d}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{B}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(r}}\right)}}\right)^{2}},

then, for all
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}\in(0,1]
, there is an
\varepsilon_{\text{S}}
-approximate sampler for
\mu_{\text{WR}}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},r,\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}}\right)}
with running time in
\mathrm{poly}\big({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{S}}}}\big)
.

Note that the role of the allocation
\Phi
in our sampling algorithms is fundamentally different than in our approximations for the partition function.
For approximating the partition function, it is sufficient to know that a suitable allocation for a given point set
X
exists. In contrast,
Lemma

7.4
, which is a core component of our sampling method, actually requires an explicit representation of
\Phi
in order to sample for each
x\in X
from
\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
.
This raises the question whether there is a way to circumvent this.

8
Concentration of random discretizations

In this section we investigate the behavior of discretizations of a hard-constraint point process
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
based on uniformly random finite point sets
X\subseteq\mathds{V}
.
We show sufficient conditions for the partition function of such random discretizations to get concentrated around the partition function of the continuous point process for a polynomial number of points.

The first part of this section focuses on how such a concentration result can be obtained from
Footnote

-0.1
.
Consequently, such an approach relies on star-convexity.

In the second part of the section, we show that a slightly weaker concentration result can be obtained without assuming star-convexity.
To this end, we first argue that the discrete partition function concentrates from below and use this to derive concentration from above.

In both cases, our sufficient condition for the concentration is closely related to the concept of a specific partitioning of
\mathds{V}
.

See
1.2

the following lemma relates the notion of a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning with our previously introduced concept of a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation.

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
be bounded and measurable with
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)>0
.
Let
\delta_{1}\in(0,1]
and
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
and assume there is a
\delta_{1}
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning of
\mathds{V}
with size
m
.
Further, let
\delta_{2}\in(0,1]
,
p\in(0,1]
, and draw
X\subset\mathds{V}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|=n\geq 48\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{2}}}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\delta_{1}}m\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2m}{p}}}\right)
uniformly at random.
With probability at least
1-p
there is a
\delta_{2}
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
.

Proof.

Let
(\mathds{V}_{i})_{i\in[m]}
be a
\delta_{1}
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning of
\mathds{V}
with size
m
.
Further, let
X\subset\mathds{V}
with
n=48\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{2}}}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\delta_{1}}m\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2m}{p}}}\right)
be drawn uniformly at random.

The main idea of the proof is to show that with probability at least
1-p
it holds that for all partitions
\mathds{V}_{i}
for
i\in[m]
the number of points from
X
that are in
\mathds{V}_{i}
is close to
\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{i}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}\cdot\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|
, and especially all partitions contain at least one point from
X
.
If this is the case, we can construct an allocation by fairly allocating each partition
\mathds{V}_{i}
to the points in
X\cap\mathds{V}_{i}
while ignoring any distance constraints.
As we argue below, this always results in an allocation with the desired properties.

For all
i\in[m]
, let
X_{i}=X\cap\mathds{V}_{i}
and let
Y_{i}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X_{i}}}\right|
.
We now show that, with probability at least
1-p
, it holds that

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\frac{\delta_{2}}{4}}}\right)\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y_{i}}}}\right]\leq Y_{i}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\frac{\delta_{2}}{4}}}\right)\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y_{i}}}}\right]

(17)

for all
i\in[m]
.
To prove this claim, note that for each
i\in[m]
the random variable
Y_{i}
follows a binomial distribution with
n
trials and success probability
\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{i}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}
.
Applying Chernoff’s inequality yields

\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|Y_{i}-\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y_{i}}}}\right]}}\right|\geq\frac{\delta_{2}}{4}}}}\right]\leq 2\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\delta_{2}^{2}}{48}\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y_{i}}}}\right]}.

Further, note that we have
\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y_{i}}}}\right]=n\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{i}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}\geq\delta_{1}\frac{n}{m}
, which gives us

\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|Y_{i}-\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y_{i}}}}\right]}}\right|\geq\frac{\delta_{2}}{4}}}}\right]\leq 2\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\delta_{2}^{2}}{48}\delta_{1}\frac{n}{m}}.

By our choice of
n
, we obtain

\mathrm{Pr}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|Y_{i}-\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y_{i}}}}\right]}}\right|\geq\frac{\delta_{2}}{4}}}}\right]\leq\frac{p}{m}.

Finally, applying union bound over
i\in[m]
proves that
equation

17
fails with probability at most
p
.

Now, let us assume
equation

17
is satisfied.
Note that this especially implies that
Y_{i}>0
.
We now construct our allocation
\Phi
as follows.
For each
i\in[m]
, partition
\mathds{V}_{i}
into sets
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{i}^{(j)}}}\right)_{j\in[Y_{i}]}
such that all
\mathds{V}_{i}^{(j)}
are measurable and have the same volume.
Note that such a division of
\mathds{V}_{i}
always exists.
Now, for each such
\mathds{V}_{i}^{(j)}
, fix exactly one
x\in X_{i}
and set
\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y}}\right)=x
for all
y\in\mathds{V}_{i}^{(j)}
.
It remains to argue that
\Phi
is a
\delta_{2}
-
\varepsilon
-allocation.
To this end, choose some
x\in X
and assume that
x\in X_{i}
for some
i\in[m]
.
Further, let
y\in\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)
and observe that by construction both,
x
and
y
, are in
\mathds{V}_{i}
.
Thus, we have

d\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x,y}}\right)\leq\varepsilon.

Moreover, because
\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y_{i}}}}\right]=n\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{i}}}\right)}{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}
we have that
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{i}}}\right)=\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y_{i}}}}\right]\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{n}
.
Thus, by
equation

17
, we obtain

\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)}}\right)=\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}_{i}}}\right)}{Y_{i}}=\frac{\mathrm{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{Y_{i}}}}\right]}{Y_{i}}\cdot\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{n}\leq\frac{1}{1-\delta_{2}/4}\cdot\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{n}\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\delta_{2}}}\right)\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{n}

and analogously

\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(x}}\right)}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{1+\delta_{2}/4}\cdot\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{n}\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1-\delta_{2}}}\right)\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{m},

which concludes the proof.
∎

8.1
Concentration with star-convexity

Assuming that the considered region is star-convex, we can combine
Footnotes

-0.1
and
8.1
to obtain the following probabilistic bound on the difference between the partition function of a hard-constraint point process and the hard-core partition function of a random discretization.

Corollary 8.2
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types, and assume
\mathds{V}\subset\mathds{R}^{d}
is star-convex.
Further, set
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
and set
R_{\min}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}\{R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\mid R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0\}
.
Let
\varepsilon\in(0,R_{\min}/2]
and
\delta_{1}\in(0,1]
and assume there is a
\delta_{1}
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning of
\mathds{V}
size
m
.
For any
\delta_{2}\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(0,\frac{1}{2}}}\right]
,
p\in(0,1]
and
n\in\mathds{N}
with

\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{4\lambda_{\max}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right),48\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{2}}}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\delta_{1}}m\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2m}{p}}}\right)}}\right\}\leq n

draw
X\subset\mathds{V}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|=n
uniformly at random and let
(G_{X},\lambda_{X})
be the corresponding discretization of
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
.
It holds that

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)-Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)}}\right|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(2\delta_{2}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{4\varepsilon}{R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}}}\right)\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}-1}}\right)Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)

with probability at least
1-p
.

Corollary

8.2
follows immediately from
Footnote

-0.1
and
Lemma

8.1
.
Note that
Corollary

8.2
means that, if we can construct
\delta_{1}
-
\varepsilon
-partitionings for some constant
\delta_{1}>0
and arbitrarily small
\varepsilon>0
, we can use this to derive a concentration result for the hard-core partition function of discretizations based on random point sets.
A general statement for this is given in the following corollary.

\randomdiscretizationconcentration

A more precise statement can be obtained when looking at specific regions of
d
-dimensional Euclidean space.
To demonstrate this, we once again consider cubic regions
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
For such regions, we have the following observation.

Lemma 8.3
.

Let
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
.
For all
\varepsilon\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
there is a
1
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning of
\mathds{V}
of size
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\sqrt{d}\frac{\ell}{\varepsilon}}}\right\rceil^{d}
.

Proof.

Set
a=\frac{\varepsilon}{\sqrt{d}}
and divide
\mathds{V}
into disjoint hypercubes of side length
a^{\prime}=\ell\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\frac{\ell}{a}}}\right\rceil^{-1}
.
Note that
\frac{\ell}{a^{\prime}}\in\mathds{N}
.
Formally, this gives the partitioning

\mathds{V}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{d}}=\prod_{j\in[d]}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[i_{j}a^{\prime},(i_{j}+1)a^{\prime}}}\right)

for
(i_{1},\dots,i_{d})\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,\dots,\frac{\ell}{a^{\prime}}-1}}\right\}^{d}
.
Obviously, this results in a partitioning of size
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\sqrt{d}\frac{\ell}{\varepsilon}}}\right\rceil^{d}
.
Further, each partition has the same volume and their diameter is upper bounded by
\sqrt{d}a^{\prime}\leq\sqrt{d}a=\varepsilon
, which proves that the partitioning is a
1
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

Note that the partitioning constructed in the proof of
Lemma

8.3
can in fact be generated by taking the canonical point set
X_{\rho}
for the smallest feasible resolution
\rho\geq\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon}
and taking the pre-image of the
0
-
\varepsilon
-allocation
\Phi
, constructed in the proof of
Lemma

5.1
, for each point in
X_{\rho}
.
This is not a coincidence.
In fact, for every point set
X
with a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation we can construct a
(1-\delta)
-
2\varepsilon
-partitioning, which, together with
Lemma

8.3
, shows an interesting equivalence between both concepts.
The results in this section could as well be stated in terms of allocations.
However, as we are not interested in discretizations for specific point sets, it is more natural to state our results in terms of partitionings.

Corollary 8.5
.

Let
(\mathds{V},R,\lambda)
be a hard-constraint point process with
q\in\mathds{N}_{\geq 1}
particle types and
\mathds{V}=[0,\ell)^{d}
for some
\ell\in\mathds{R}_{>0}
. Let
X\subset\mathds{V}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|=n
, chosen uniformly at random.
For all
\varepsilon_{\text{D}}\in(0,1]
and
p\in(0,1]
, there exists an
n_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}},p}\in\upTheta\big({\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{4}\varepsilon_{\text{D}}^{-3}\ln\big(\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}p}\big)}\big)
such that for all
n\geq n_{\varepsilon_{\text{D}},p}
, with probability at least
1-p
it holds that

\mathrm{e}^{-\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)\leq Z_{\text{HC}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(G_{X},\lambda_{X}}}\right)\leq\mathrm{e}^{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}Z\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V},R,\lambda}}\right)
.

Proof.

Set
\lambda_{\max}=\max_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)
and
R_{\min}=\min_{i,j\in[q]}\{R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)\mid R\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i,j}}\right)>0\}
.
Note that by
Lemma

8.3
we know that for

m=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\lceil\sqrt{d}\ell\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{48q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\frac{4}{R_{\min}}}}\right\}}}\right\rceil^{d}

there is a
1
-
\varepsilon
-partitioning of
\mathds{V}
with size
m
for some

\varepsilon\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{48q\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\lambda_{\max},\lambda_{\max}^{2}}}\right\}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}}}\right)^{\frac{1}{d}}\min\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1,\frac{R_{\min}}{4}}}\right\}.

Set

\delta=\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{8q\lambda_{\max}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}

and let

n\geq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1}{\delta}}}\right)^{2}m\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{2m}{\Phi}}}\right)\in\upTheta\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)^{4}}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}^{3}}\ln\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)}{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}p}}}\right)}}}\right).

Due to
Lemma

8.1
we know that for
X\subset\mathds{V}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|=n
uniformly at random there is a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
with probability at least
1-p
.

The rest of the proof is mostly similar to the proof of
Theorem

5.2
.
Let
X\subset\mathds{V}
with
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|=n
such that a
\delta
-
\varepsilon
-allocation for
X
exists.
For
\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
sufficiently large we have that
\varepsilon\leq\frac{R_{\min}}{2}
,
\delta\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|=n\geq 4\lambda_{\max}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)
.
Thus, we can apply
Footnote

-0.1
to
X
.
Now, observe that

\displaystyle\frac{8}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|X}}\right|}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)^{2}\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{6}

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{4R_{\min}}}}\right)^{d}\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{12}

\displaystyle 2\delta\sum_{i\in[q]}\lambda\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(i}}\right)\nu\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathds{V}}}\right)\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\text{D}}}{12}.

This yields
